\displaystyle Y_{N}^{i}(r)=S^{i}(r)^{2}-\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[\big(S^{i}(s)-S^{i}(s-1)\big)^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\big]=S^{i}(r)^{2}-4\mu_{\nu_{N}}(1-\mu_{\nu_{N}})r

are
\mathbf{P}_{N}
-martingales. Additionally, the processes for
1\leq i<j\leq k
given by

\displaystyle K_{N}^{ij}(r)

\displaystyle=S^{i}(r)S^{j}(r)-\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[\big(S^{i}(s)-S^{i}(s-1)\big)\big(S^{j}(s)-S^{j}(s-1)\big)\big|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\big]

\displaystyle=S^{i}(r)S^{j}(r)-4\sigma_{\nu_{N}}^{2}V_{r}^{ij}

are
\mathbf{P}_{N}
-martingales. Rescaling, we find that the processes

(3.6)

\displaystyle N^{-1}Y^{i}_{N}(Nt)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}^{i}_{N}(t)^{2}-4\mu_{\nu_{N}}(1-\mu_{\nu_{N}})t

(3.7)

\displaystyle N^{-1}K^{ij}_{N}(Nt)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{N}^{i}(t)\mathbf{X}_{N}^{j}(t)-N^{-1/2}4\sigma_{\nu_{N}}^{2}\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(t)

are
\mathbf{P}_{N}
martingales indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.
Using Lemma
2.6
we find that these martingales are uniformly integrable as well.

Proof of
(i)
.
Now we pass this information to the limit points. Martingality is preserved by limit points as long as one has uniform integrability, thus it is clear that
\mathbf{U}
is necessarily a
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
-martingale (with respect to the canonical filtration on
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k(k-1)/2})
). Next, note that
\lim_{N\to\infty}4\mu_{\nu_{N}}(1-\mu_{\nu_{N}})=1
by our assumption on
\mu_{\nu_{N}}
. Again using that martingality is preserved by limit points, we can thus apply (
3.6
) to conclude that
\mathbf{U}^{i}(t)^{2}-t
is a
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
-martingale, i.e.,
\mathbf{U}^{i}
necessarily has quadratic variation
t
. Yet again using the fact that martingality is preserved by limit points, we can use (
3.7
) and tightness in
C[0,T]
of the processes
\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}
to conclude that for all
i<j
, the process
\mathbf{U}^{i}\mathbf{U}^{j}
is a
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
-martingale, i.e.,
\langle\mathbf{U}^{i},\mathbf{U}^{j}\rangle=0
for
i\neq j
. By Levy’s characterization, we may conclude that
\mathbf{U}
is therefore a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. This proves
(i)
.

Proof of
(ii)
.
We will again use a martingale problem.
Recall the martingales
\mathcal{M}(r)
from (
2.4
). Rescaling, we find that

|\mathbf{X}_{N}^{i}(t)-\mathbf{X}_{N}^{j}(t)|-4\big[(\mu_{\nu_{N}}(1-\mu_{\nu_{N}})-\sigma_{\nu_{N}}^{2})\big]\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(t)

are
\mathbf{P}_{N}
-martingales indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.
All quantities are uniformly integrable and tight in
C[0,T]
as
N\to\infty
(as explained earlier). By our hypothesis on
\nu_{N}
, we have
\mu_{\nu_{N}}(1-\mu_{\nu_{N}})\to 1/4
. Thus for the limit points, we conclude that the processes
|\mathbf{U}^{i}-\mathbf{U}^{j}|-(1-4\sigma^{2})\mathscr{L}^{ij}
are
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
-martingales. As
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion, and the processes
\mathscr{L}^{ij}
are
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
-a.s. nondecreasing, we conclude by Tanaka’s formula that
(1-4\sigma^{2})\mathscr{L}^{ij}
is the local time at zero of
U^{i}-U^{j}
. This uniquely identifies the law of the joint limit point
(\mathbf{U},(\mathscr{L}^{ij})_{i<j})
.

Proof of
(iii)
We now show that as
N\to\infty
the processes
\mathscr{T}_{N}
converge in probability to the zero process with respect to the topology of
C[0,T]
. Indeed these processes are tight because for
\delta>0
,

\sup_{|t-s|<\delta}|\mathscr{T}_{N}(t)-\mathscr{T}_{N}(s)|\leq\sup_{|t-s|<\delta}N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}V^{ij}(Nt)-V^{ij}(Ns)

and we already know that the intersection time process on the right side is tight. Now if we take any
joint
limit point
(F_{t},\mathbf{U}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
of the pair of processes
(\mathscr{T}_{N},\mathbf{X}_{N})
, then
F_{t}
is a nondecreasing process with
F_{0}=0
. Furthermore by definition of the set
\Gamma^{(\leq k-2)}
(see (
2.2
)), this limit point
F
is constant on every connected component of the complement of the random closed set
K\cup L
where

\displaystyle\text{$K:=\{t\in[0,T]:\mathbf{U}^{i}_{t}=\mathbf{U}^{j}_{t}=\mathbf{U}^{p}_{t}$ for some $1\leq i<j<p\leq k\}$},

L:=\{t\in[0,T]:\mathbf{U}^{i}_{t}=\mathbf{U}^{j}_{t}
and
\mathbf{U}^{p}_{t}=\mathbf{U}^{q}_{t}
for some
i<j
and
p<q
with
\{i,j\}\cap\{p,q\}=\emptyset\}.

We already know that
\mathbf{U}
is a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. But 3d Brownian motion never hits the diagonal
\{x=y=z\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
after time
0
, and likewise, a 4d Brownian motion never hits the proper 2d linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
given by
\{x=y\}\cap\{z=w\}
after time
0
. Consequently
K\cup L=\{0\},
and so
F_{t}=0
identically.

Proof of
(iv)
Let us now take any
\mathcal{G}_{N}
satisfying the assumption of the theorem. We first prove that the linearly interpolated processes
\big(\mathcal{G}_{N}(Nt)\big)_{t\in[0,T]\cap N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
are tight in
C[0,T]
. Indeed, by Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, we have for all
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
that

\mathbf{E}\big[|\mathcal{G}_{N}(Nt)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(Ns)|^{p}\big]\leq\mathbf{E}\bigg[\bigg([\mathcal{G}_{N}](Nt)-[\mathcal{G}_{N}](Ns)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\bigg]\leq C\cdot\mathbf{E}\bigg[\bigg(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i<j}\big(V^{ij}(Nt)-V^{ij}(Ns)\big)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\bigg]\!.\!

The second inequality above is due to the assumption (
3.2
). Lemma
2.7
implies that the term on the right-hand side of the above equation is bounded by
C|t-s|^{p/4}
for some constant
C>0
free of
N
. This verifies the tightness of
t\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{N}(Nt)
.

Consider any limit point of the triple
\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{N},\;\mathbf{X}_{N},\;(1-4\sigma^{2})\big(\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\big)
, say
(\mathcal{G},\mathbf{U},\big(L^{ij}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k})
whose law
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
(now a different object from the previous parts of the proof) we view as a measure on the canonical space
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k(k-1)/2})
. By the tightness and
L^{p}
bounds above, the process
\mathcal{G}
is still a
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
-martingale with respect to the canonical filtration on this space. We claim that the quadratic covariations satisfy
\langle G,\mathbf{U}^{i}\rangle_{t}=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
. To prove this, note that in the prelimit,
\mathbf{X}^{i}_{N}(t)\mathcal{G}_{N}(Nt)-\mathscr{P}_{N}(t)
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is a
\mathbf{P}_{N}
-martingale where

\mathscr{P}_{N}(t):=N^{-1/2}\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{N}[(\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s-1))(R^{i}(s)-R^{i}(s-1)-(2\mu_{\nu_{N}}-1))|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}].

Note that

\displaystyle|\mathscr{P}_{N}(t)|

\displaystyle=N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{s\in[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-1)}}\big|\mathbf{E}_{N}[(\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s-1))(R^{i}(s)-R^{i}(s-1)-(2\mu_{\nu_{N}}-1)|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}]\big|

\displaystyle\leq N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{s\in[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-1)}}\bigg[\sqrt{\mathbf{E}_{N}[(\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s-1))^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}]}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\cdot\sqrt{\mathbf{E}_{N}[(R^{i}(s)-R^{i}(s-1)-(2\mu_{\nu_{N}}-1))^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}]}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s\in[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-1)}}C^{1/2}N^{-3/4}\leq C^{1/2}N^{-1/4}\sum_{i<j}\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}.

Let us briefly explain the above deductions. The first equality is due to the fact that
\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s-1)=0
for
s\in\Gamma^{(k)}
via assumption (
3.2
). The inequality in the second line follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Note that
R^{i}(s)-R^{i}(s-1)-(2\mu_{\nu_{N}}-1)
is uniformly bounded by
2
. Thus, employing the bound in (
3.2
), we get the inequality in the third line. The final inequality follows from the definition of
\Gamma^{(k-1)}
and
\mathscr{V}^{ij}
.

Now, since we already know
\mathscr{V}_{N}
are tight, we conclude that
|\mathscr{P}_{N}(t)|\to 0
. Invoking Lemmas
2.6
and
2.8
, we see that
\mathbf{X}_{N}^{i}\mathcal{G}_{N}-\mathscr{P}_{N}
is uniformly integrable. Hence
\mathbf{U}^{i}\mathcal{G}
, being a limit point of
\mathbf{X}_{N}^{i}\mathcal{G}_{N}-\mathscr{P}_{N}
, is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{lim}}
-martingale for all
1\leq i\leq k
, so that
\langle\mathcal{G},\mathbf{U}^{i}\rangle_{t}=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
, as claimed. In fact, Proposition
2.8
guarantees that the process
\exp(\mathcal{G}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle)
is a
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
-martingale. Now a standard argument that we used in our previous paper
[
DDP24
, Proof of Theorem 3.2]
ensures that
\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}[\exp(\mathcal{G}(T)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(T))\mid\mathcal{F}_{T}(\mathbf{U})]=1.
For completeness, we reproduce the argument in the next paragraph.

Take any bounded
\mathcal{F}_{T}(\mathbf{U})
-measurable functional
H:C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathbb{R}
. Note that the process
t\mapsto\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}[H(\mathbf{U})|\mathcal{F}_{t}(\mathbf{U})]
is a martingale in the filtration of
\mathbf{U}
. Under
\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}},
the marginal law of
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion by the result of part
(i)
, thus by the martingale representation theorem we have

H(\mathbf{U})=\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}[H(\mathbf{U})]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{0}^{T}h_{s}^{i}dU_{s}^{i}

for some adapted
\mathbb{R}
-valued processes
h^{1},\ldots,h^{k}
. For stochastic exponential we have

\exp\left(\mathcal{G}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(T)\right)=1+\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(\mathcal{G}(s)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(s)\right)d\mathcal{G}(s).

Using this we find that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}\left[\big(e^{\mathcal{G}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(T)}-1\big)\big(H(\mathbf{U})-\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}[H(\mathbf{U})]\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}e^{\mathcal{G}(s)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(s)}d\mathcal{G}(s)\bigg)\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}h_{s}^{i}dU^{i}_{s}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}h_{s}^{i}e^{\mathcal{G}(s)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(s)}d\langle\mathcal{G},U^{i}\rangle(s)\bigg]=0,

where the last equality is due to the fact
\langle U^{i},\mathcal{G}\rangle\equiv 0
almost surely. This proves that for all bounded measurable
H
we have
\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}[e^{\mathcal{G}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(T)}H(\mathbf{U})]=\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}[H(\mathbf{U})]
, establishing (
3.5
).

Proof of
(v)
By assumption (
3.2
), we have
|\mathcal{G}_{N}(r+1)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(r)|\leq C^{1/2}N^{-1/4}\leq 1
deterministically for large enough
N
. For
x\in[-1,1]
we have
e^{x}\leq 1+x+x^{2}
, therefore we find that

e^{\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s-1)}\leq 1+(\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s-1))+(\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s-1))^{2},

consequently using martingality of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
and
\log(1+u)\leq u
we find that

\displaystyle W_{N}(r)-W_{N}(r-1)

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathbf{E}_{N}[e^{\mathcal{G}_{N}(r)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(r-1)}|\mathcal{F}_{r-1}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{N}[(\mathcal{G}_{N}(r)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(r-1))^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{r-1}]\leq CN^{-1/2}\sum_{i<j}(V^{ij}(r)-V^{ij}(r-1)),

where we used (
3.2
). Using this bound together with the nondecreasing property of
W_{N}
, and Lemmas
2.6
and
2.7
, it is clear that for all
p\geq 0

\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{N}[|W_{N}(Nt)-W_{N}(Ns)|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq C|t-s|^{p/2},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{N}[e^{pW_{N}(Nt)}]<\infty.

Thus the linearly interpolated processes
t\mapsto W_{N}(Nt)
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
are tight in
C[0,T]
, so we may consider any limit point
\mathcal{W}
which is taken
jointly
with the earlier triple of processes
(\mathcal{G},\mathbf{U},\big(L^{ij}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k})
. Note that since
\exp(\mathcal{G}_{N}-W_{N})
are martingales in the prelimit, and the above exponential bound on
W_{N}
guarantees uniform integrability of its exponential, it follows that
\exp(\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{W})
is a martingale for any limit point of the joint 4-tuple. Since
\mathcal{G}
is a continuous martingale, this then forces that
\mathcal{W}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle
.
∎

We record an extension of the above theorem which also takes certain types of measures into account. The following corollary will be useful in our later analysis.

Corollary 3.2
.

Assume the same notations and assumptions of Theorem
3.1
.
Suppose
k=2m
is even. For
t>s>0
define

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}(s,t):=\{(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m})\in[s,t]^{m}\mid s\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq\cdots\leq s_{m}\leq t\}

to be the set of all
m
ordered points in
[s,t]
. We define
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{m}(0,T))
to be the space of finite and non-negative Borel measures on that simplex, equipped with the topology of weak convergence. Consider the following sequence of
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{m}(0,T))
-valued random variables

(3.9)

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}:=N^{-m/2}(1-4\sigma^{2})^{m}\sum_{u_{1}\leq...\leq u_{m}\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,T]}\;\;\;\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}^{2j-1}_{N}(u_{j})=\mathbf{X}^{2j}_{N}(u_{j})\}}\delta_{(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m})}

where
\mathbf{X}_{N}=(\mathbf{X}^{1}_{N},\ldots,\mathbf{X}^{k}_{N})
. The random variables
\{\gamma_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight in
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{m}(0,T))
. Moreover, any limit point of the triple
(\mathbf{X}_{N},\;(1-4\sigma^{2})\big(\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\;\gamma_{N})
is of the form

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{U},\;\big(L^{ij}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\;\prod_{j=1}^{m}dL^{{2j-1,2j}}(u_{j})\right)

where
\mathbf{U},L^{ij}
are as in Theorem
3.1
, and
dL(t)
denotes the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure induced by the increasing function
t\mapsto L(t)
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{m}(0,T))\leq\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left[N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{u\in[0,T]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}_{N}^{2j-1}(u)=\mathbf{X}_{N}^{2j}(u)\}}\right].

From Lemma
2.6
we know the exponential moments for each of the terms in the product are uniformly bounded under the
\mathbf{E}_{N}
measure. Thus for all
p\geq 1
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{N}[\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{m}(0,T))^{p}]<\infty.

Hence the laws of
\{\gamma_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight, because the total mass of
\gamma_{N}
is a tight family of random variables and because
\Delta_{m}(0,T)
as defined in (
3.8
) is a compact space. Let
\left(\mathbf{U},\big(K^{ij}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\gamma\right)
be any limit point of the sequence
(\mathbf{X}_{N},(1-4\sigma^{2})\big(\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\gamma_{N})
. Note that the joint cumulative distribution function of the measure
\gamma
is necessarily given by a product of the
K^{ij}
. Since we know that
K^{ij}=L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}
by Theorem
3.1
(ii)
, this implies that
\gamma=\prod_{j=1}^{m}dL_{0}^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(u_{j})
.
∎

3.1.
Convergence of moments

We will now prove that moments of the field (
1.3
) converge to the moments of (
1.4
). It will be a consequence of Theorem
3.1
, illustrating the applicability of the convergence theorem. While the moment convergence is not enough to prove the weak convergence of Theorem
1.1
, it gives a strong indication of it, and more importantly, it implies some useful estimates that will be used later.

Proposition 3.3

(Moment convergence)
.

Fix
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
t>0
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. With
\mathscr{U}_{N}
as defined in (
1.3
), we have that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\phi)^{k}]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)\phi(x)dx\bigg)^{k}\bigg],

where
(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)
solves (
1.4
) with initial condition
\delta_{0}(x).

Proof.

Fix any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. With
\mathscr{U}_{N}
defined in (
1.3
), we wish to compute the annealed expectation
\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\phi)^{k}]
. Just like (
1.6
) we can write

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\phi)^{k}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}^{\omega}_{(k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(R^{j}(Nt)-N^{3/4}t)}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{j}(Nt)-N^{3/4}t)\big)\bigg],

where
\mathsf{E}^{\omega}_{(k)}
is a
quenched
expectation of
k
independent particles sampled from a fixed realization of the environment
(\omega_{t,x})_{t\geq 0,x\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Taking the annealed expectation over the quenched one we get

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\phi)^{k}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu}^{(k)}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(R^{j}({Nt})-N^{3/4}t)}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{j}({Nt})-N^{3/4}t)\big)\bigg].

Let
\{\nu_{N}^{*}\}_{N\geq 1}
be the skewed sequence of measures corresponding to
\nu
. Recall the measure
\mathbf{Q}_{RW_{\nu}^{(k)}}^{\lambda}
,
\mathpzc M^{\lambda}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{N}
from Definition
2.4
, (
2.7
) and (
2.9
) respectively. The key idea from here is to get rid of divergent terms appearing in the above expectation through the following change of variable:

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu}^{(k)}}[\mathbf{F}]=\mathbf{Q}_{RW_{\nu}^{(k)}}^{N^{-1/4}}\left[\frac{1}{\mathpzc M^{N^{-\frac{1}{4}}}(Nt)}\mathbf{F}\right]=\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu_{N}^{*}}^{(k)}}\left[\frac{1}{\mathpzc M^{N^{-\frac{1}{4}}}(Nt)}e^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}_{N}}(Nt)}\mathbf{F}\right].

where
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{R})
is any measurable functional on the path space.
Then we appeal to Theorem
3.1
to deduce convergence under the measure
\mathbf{P}_{RW_{\nu_{N}^{*}}^{(k)}}
.

Indeed, applying the tilts in (
3.12
) we get

(3.13)

\displaystyle\mbox{r.h.s.~of \eqref{e.utphi}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu_{N}^{*}}^{(k)}}\bigg[\exp\bigg({\mathcal{G}}_{N}(Nt)-\sum_{s\leq Nt}\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu_{N}}^{(k)}}[e^{\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s-1)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-1}]\bigg)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{1}{\mathpzc M^{N^{-\frac{1}{4}}}(NT)}\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{N,t,N^{-\frac{1}{2}}(R^{j}({Nt})-N^{\frac{3}{4}}t)}\phi\big(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}(R^{j}({Nt})-N^{\frac{3}{4}}t)\big)\bigg]\!.\!

Here
\mathcal{G}_{N}
is a family of
\mathbf{P}_{RW_{\nu}^{(k)}}
-martingales satisfying (
2.10
). We now use the notations of rescaled processes:
\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathscr{V}_{N}^{ij},
and
\mathscr{T}_{N}
, considered in (
3.1
). We claim that

(3.14)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathpzc M^{N^{-1/4}}(NT)}\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(R^{j}(Nt)-N^{3/4}t)}=\exp\left(4\sigma^{2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(t)-\mathsf{Err}_{N}(t)\right)

where

(3.15)

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Err}_{N}(t)|\leq\mathrm{C}\bigg(\mathscr{T}_{N}(t)+N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(t)\bigg).

The proofs of (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) follow by some algebraic calculations and definition chasing. We shall prove them in a moment. Note that
\nu_{N}
and
\mathcal{G}_{N}
satisfy the hypothesis of Theorem
3.1
.
Thus applying Theorem
3.1
, in view of the bound in (
3.15
), we see that any limit point of the random variable inside the expectation on the r.h.s. of (
3.13
) is of the form

\displaystyle\exp\left(\mathcal{G}(t)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(t)\right)\exp\left(\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}L^{ij}(t)\right)\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi({U}^{i}(t)),

where
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion on
[0,T]
, defined on a standard probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbf{P})
,

L^{ij}(t):=L^{U^{i}-U^{j}}_{0}(t)
is the local time at zero of
U_{i}-U_{j}
accrued by time
t
and
\mathcal{G}
is a
C[0,T]
-valued random variable satisfying
\mathbf{E}[e^{p{\mathcal{G}}(T)-\frac{p}{2}\langle{\mathcal{G}}\rangle(T)}]<\infty
for all
p>0
as well as (
3.5
).

Thanks to the estimates in Lemma
2.6
and Proposition
2.8
, we see that the random variables in the prelimit are uniformly integrable. Thus every subsequence of the expectation on r.h.s. of (
3.13
) has a further subsequence which as
N\to\infty
converges to

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left(\mathcal{G}(t)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(t)\right)\exp\left(\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}L^{ij}(t)\right)\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(\mathbf{U}^{i}(t))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}L^{ij}(t)\right)\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(\mathbf{U}^{i}(t))\right].

The last equality is due to (
3.5
). Since this expression is free
\mathcal{G}
(which may depend on the subsequence), we thus get that

(3.16)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{\mathbb{E}}[\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\phi)^{k}]=\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}L^{ij}(t)\right)\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(\mathbf{U}^{i}(t))\right].

By the Feynman-Kac formula, the solution
\mathcal{U}
of the stochastic heat equation (
1.4
) admits well-known moment formulas in terms of local times of Brownian bridges. In particular, from
[
BC95
, Lemma 2.3]
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{U}_{t}(x_{i})\right]=p(t,x_{1})\cdots p(t,x_{k})\mathbf{E}_{\vec{x}}\bigg[e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i<j}L^{B^{i}-B^{j}}_{0}(t)}\bigg],

where
p
is the standard heat kernel, and the
B^{i}
are independent Brownian bridges on
[0,t]
from
0
to
x_{i}
respectively. Note that in
[
BC95
]
, local time is defined as an integral against Lebesgue measure:
L^{X}_{a}(t):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{\{a-\varepsilon<X_{s}<a+\varepsilon\}}ds
, in contrast with our definition of local time in (
3.4
) which is an integral against
d\langle X,X\rangle_{s}
. Hence, when taking the local time of
B^{i}-B^{j}
, our definition of local time differs from theirs by a factor of
2
.

We can integrate this formula to arrive at the moment formula for
\mathcal{U}_{t}(\phi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)\phi(x)dx
, with
(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)
solving (
1.4
), and we see that it matches the right-hand side of (
3.16
), thus completing the proof of moment convergence for
\mathscr{U}_{N}
modulo (
3.14
) and (
3.15
).

Let us now prove (
3.14
)-(
3.15
). Using the definition of martingale
\mathpzc M^{\lambda}
from (
2.7
) we see that

(3.17)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathpzc M^{N^{-\frac{1}{4}}}(Nt)}\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{N,t,N^{-\frac{1}{2}}(R^{j}(Nt)-N^{3/4}t)}=\exp\bigg(\sum_{\ell=0}^{Nt-1}f_{\ell}^{N^{-\frac{1}{4}},k}-kNt\log\cosh(N^{-\frac{1}{4}})\bigg),

where
f_{\ell}^{N^{-1/4},k}
is defined in (
2.8
). Recall
\Gamma^{(v)}
from (
2.2
). Using the expressions for
f_{\ell}^{\lambda,k}
for
\ell\in\Gamma^{(k)},\Gamma^{(k-1)}
(obtained after (
2.8
)) we have

(3.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{Nt-1}f_{\ell}^{N^{-\frac{1}{4}},k}-kNt\log\cosh(N^{-\frac{1}{4}})

\displaystyle=g(N^{-1/4},\sigma^{2})\cdot\#\{[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(k-1)}\}+\sum_{\ell\in[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-2)}}f_{\ell}^{N^{-1/4},k}

\displaystyle-k\log\cosh(N^{-1/4})\cdot\#\{[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-2)}\}.

We note that

(3.19)

\displaystyle\#\{[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(k-1)}\}=N^{1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathscr{V}_{N}^{ij}(t)-\sum_{\ell\in[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-2)}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{i}(\ell)=R^{j}(\ell)\}},

where
\mathscr{V}_{N}^{ij}
is defined in (
3.1
). Let us now define

\displaystyle\mathsf{Err}_{N}^{(1)}(t)

\displaystyle:=g(N^{-1/4},\sigma^{2})\sum_{\ell\in[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-2)}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{i}(\ell)=R^{j}(\ell)\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\sum_{\ell\in[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-2)}}f_{\ell}^{N^{-1/4},k}+k\log\cosh(N^{-1/4})\cdot\#\{[Nt]\cap\Gamma^{(\leq k-2)}\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Err}_{N}^{(2)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\big(g(N^{-1/4},\sigma^{2})-4\sigma^{2}N^{-1/2}\big)N^{1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathscr{V}_{N}^{ij}(t).

In view of the relation in (
3.19
) and above definitions, we obtain from (
3.18
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{Nt-1}f_{\ell}^{N^{-\frac{1}{4}},k}-kNt\log\cosh(N^{-\frac{1}{4}})=4\sigma^{2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathscr{V}_{N}^{ij}(t)+\mathsf{Err}_{N}^{(2)}(t)-\mathsf{Err}_{N}^{(1)}(t).

Setting
\mathsf{Err}_{N}(t):=\mathsf{Err}_{N}^{(2)}(t)-\mathsf{Err}_{N}^{(1)}(t)
leads to (
3.14
) from (
3.17
) and the above relation. To prove the bound in (
3.15
), note that
g(N^{-1/4},\sigma^{2})=4\sigma^{2}N^{-1/2}+O(N^{-1})
. Thus we have

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Err}_{N}^{(2)}(t)|\leq\mathrm{C}N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathscr{V}_{N}^{ij}(t).

Furthermore
\log\cosh(N^{-1/4})=O(N^{-1/2})
and
f_{\ell}^{N^{-1/4},k}
is uniformly bounded by
\mathrm{C}N^{-1/2}
by Lemma
2.3
. Using these bounds and recalling the definition of
\mathscr{T}_{N}
from (
3.1
) we see that

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Err}_{N}^{(1)}(t)|\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\mathscr{T}_{N}(t).

Combining the bounds in the last two displays verifies (
3.15
).
∎

4.
A discrete Hopf-Cole transform: Proof overview of Theorem
1.1

Having established the moment convergence, in this section, we present a technical roadmap to the proof of the main result Theorem
1.1
. We will rely on the martingale characterization of the solution of the multiplicative noise stochastic heat equation. Specifically, consider a measure
\mu
on
C([0,T],C(\mathbb{R}))
, and let
(\mathcal{U}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
denote the canonical process on that space. The canonical filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}
on
C([0,T],C(\mathbb{R}))
is the one generated by
\{\mathcal{U}(s):s\leq t\}
. Define the heat operator
\mathcal{L}:=\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}
, which acts on tempered distributions
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by the formula
((\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f,\varphi)=(f,(-\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})\varphi)
.

Table 1.
Objects that will appear in the proof of weak convergence

Objects

Discrete Version

Continuous Version

Heat Operator

\mathcal{L}_{N}
(
4.4
)

\mathcal{L}=\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}

Microscopic version

Rescaled field

Continuum limit

Process of interest

{Z}_{N}^{\omega}
(
4.5
)

\mathscr{U}_{N}
(
1.3
)

\mathcal{U}
(
1.4
)

Relevant martingale

{v}_{N}
(
4.6
)

M_{N}
(
4.9
)

\mathcal{Y}
(
4.1
)

Quadratic Martingale field

Q_{N}
(
4.14
)

Integrated
\mathcal{U}^{2}

We recall a result of
[
BG97
, Proposition 4.11]
inspired by the work of
[
KS88
]
. If for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the processes

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(t,\phi):=\mathcal{U}(t,\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(\mathcal{U}(s),\phi^{\prime\prime})ds

(which is formally the same as
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}\mathcal{U})(s,y)\phi(y)dyds
) are
(\mathcal{F}_{t},\mu)
-martingales with quadratic variation

(4.2)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}(\cdot,\phi)\rangle(t)={\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}(\mathcal{U}(s)^{2},\phi^{2})_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}ds,

then (under reasonable assumptions on the spatial growth of
\mathcal{U}(t)
at infinity) the measure
\mu
necessarily coincides with the law of (
1.4
) started from an initial condition that is distributed as
\mathcal{U}(0)
under
\mu
.

We shall eventually show our prelimiting field from (
1.3
) is tight and any limit point satisfies the above martingale characterization. The key observation that drives our proof is that the prelimiting field itself satisfies a lattice stochastic heat equation. This will then allow us to define some observables that will be crucial in proving tightness and identifying the limit points in later sections. We now end the heuristic discussions and rigorously derive the lattice SPDE, then write out the quadratic variations of the resultant martingales.

Definition 4.1
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Define a discrete lattice

(4.3)

\Lambda_{N}:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}:x+tN^{-1/4}\in\mathbb{Z}\},

and a
discrete heat operator

\mathcal{L}_{N}
, which acts on real-valued functions
f:\Lambda_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(4.4)

(\mathcal{L}_{N}f)(t,x):=f(t+1,x-N^{-1/4})-\big(\rho_{N}f(t,x-1)+(1-\rho_{N})f(t,x+1)\big),

with
\rho_{N}
given by (
2.1
).

Recall
\mathsf{P}^{\omega}(t,x)
from (
1.1
). Assume
\mathbb{E}[\omega_{ij}]=\tfrac{1}{2}
. For each realization of the environment
\omega
, define the random function
Z_{N}^{\omega}:\Lambda_{N}\to\mathbb{R}
:

(4.5)

Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,x):=C_{N,N^{-1}t,N^{-1/2}x}\mathsf{P}^{\omega}(t,x+N^{-1/4}t),

where
C_{N,T,x}
is defined in (
1.2
). Note that
Z_{N}^{\omega}
is the pointwise version of the rescaled field
\mathscr{U}_{N}
defined in (
1.3
). Roughly speaking, Theorem
1.1
suggests that we wish to prove convergence to SHE for the fields
N^{1/2}Z^{\omega}_{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}x)
. The following lemma suggests
Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,x)
satisfies a discrete version of (
4.1
) on
\Lambda_{N}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For
(t,x)\in\Lambda_{N}
, define

(4.6)

v_{N}(t,x):=\big(\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N}^{\omega}\big)(t,x).

Then
v_{N}
is a martingale-difference field in the filtration
\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{t}=\sigma(\{\omega_{s,x}:x\in\mathbb{Z},0\leq s\leq t\}),
i.e.

(4.7)

\mathbb{E}\big[v_{N}(t,x)\big|\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{t-1}]=0.

Proof.

First note that
v_{N}(t,x)
is indeed adapted to
\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{t}
because
Z_{N}^{\omega}(t+1,\cdot)
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{t}
. Note that the quenched probabilities satisfy the relation

\mathsf{P}^{\omega}(t+1,x)=(1-\omega_{t,x+1})\mathsf{P}^{\omega}(t,x+1)+\omega_{t,x-1}\mathsf{P}^{\omega}(t,x-1),

which can be derived from conditioning on the previous step. Using the definition of
Z_{N}^{\omega}
and
C_{N,t,x}
from (
4.5
) and (
1.2
) respectively, we thus have

\displaystyle Z_{N}^{\omega}(t+1,x-N^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=e^{N^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\log\cosh(N^{-\frac{1}{4}})}\big[(1-\omega_{t,x+N^{-1/4}t+1})e^{N^{-1/4}(-1-N^{-1/4})}Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,x+1)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\omega_{t,x+N^{-1/4}t-1}e^{N^{-1/4}(1-N^{-1/4})}Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,x-1)\big]

\displaystyle=2(1-\omega_{t,x+N^{-1/4}t+1})(1-\rho_{N})Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,x+1)+2\omega_{t,x+N^{-1/4}t-1}\rho_{N}Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,x-1),

for
(t,x)\in\Lambda_{N}
. Consequently we have that

(4.8)

v_{N}(t,x)=(1-2\omega_{t,x+N^{-1/4}t+1})(1-\rho_{N})Z^{\omega}_{N}(t,x+1)+(2\omega_{t,x+N^{-1/4}t-1}-1)\rho_{N}Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,x-1).

Since the
\omega_{t,x}
have mean 1/2 and are independent of
\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{t-1}
, and since
Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{t-1}
measurable, (
4.7
) follows.
∎

Definition 4.3
.

Define the rescaled martingale field as follows. For
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
let

(4.9)

M_{N}(t,\phi):=\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)v_{N}(r,x).

M_{N}(t,\phi)
is the macroscopic field corresponding to the microscopic variables
v_{N}(t,x)
.
M_{N}(t,\phi)
is the discrete analog of
\mathcal{Y}(t,\phi)
defined in (
4.1
).
Note by (
4.7
) that
M_{N}(t,\phi)
is a martingale indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. A substantial amount of effort in this paper will be spent studying the quadratic variations of
M_{N}(t,\phi)
, as we need to verify that (
4.2
) holds for any limit point. By defining
\eta_{N}(r,x):=1-2\omega_{r,x+N^{-1/4}r}
, we can use (
4.8
) to collect neighboring terms and rewrite (
4.9
) as follows for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:

(4.10)

M_{N}(t,\phi)=\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}x\big)Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)\eta_{N}(r,x),

where

(\nabla_{N}\phi)(x):=(1-\rho_{N})\phi(x-N^{-1/2})-\rho_{N}\phi(x+N^{-1/2}).

Consequently the optional quadratic variation is given for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by

(4.11)

\displaystyle[M_{N}(\phi)]_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\bigg(\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}x\big)Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)\eta_{N}(r,x)\bigg)^{2}.

Since
\mathbb{E}[\eta_{N}(r,x)\eta_{N}(r,y)|\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{r-1}]=4\sigma^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{x=y\}}
, the predictable quadratic variation of
M_{N}(\phi)
is given for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle\langle M_{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}x\big)Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)\eta_{N}(r,x)\bigg)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{r-1}\bigg]

(4.12)

\displaystyle=4\sigma^{2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}\big[(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}x\big)Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)\big]^{2}.

Note that
M_{N}(t,\phi)^{2}-[M_{N}(\phi)](t)
and
M_{N}(t,\phi)^{2}-\langle M_{N}(\phi)\rangle(t)
are both martingales indexed by
N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, which will be relevant in later sections. We decompose (
4.12
) as

(4.13)

\displaystyle\langle M_{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{N}(t,\phi)+\big((2\rho_{N}-1)^{2}\sqrt{N}\big)Q_{N}(t,\phi^{2})

where for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle Q_{N}(t,\phi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)^{2}

(4.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathsf{E}_{(2)}^{\omega}\bigg[\phi(N^{-1/2}(S(r)-N^{-1/4}r))\mathbf{1}_{\{S(r)=R(r)\}}C_{N,N^{-1}r,N^{-1/2}(S(r)-N^{-1/4}r)}^{2}\bigg]

where
\mathsf{E}_{(2)}^{\omega}
denotes quenched expectation for two independent motions
(R(r),S(r))_{r\geq 0}
in the fixed realization of the environment
\omega
, and where
\mathcal{E}_{N}(T,\phi)
is an “error term” given by

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{N}(t,\phi):=4\sigma^{2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}\big[(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}x\big)^{2}-(2\rho_{N}-1)^{2}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)^{2}]Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)^{2}.

Definition 4.4
.

We shall call
Q_{N}(t,\phi)
the
Quadratic martingale field
(QMF), and
\mathcal{E}_{N}(t,\phi)
the
error term
.

It will be shown that
Q_{N}
contributes meaningfully in the limit, while the error term
\mathcal{E}_{N}
vanishes in the limit. Let us see why
\mathcal{E}_{N}
should vanish. Notice that a first order Taylor expansion gives
\nabla_{N}\phi(x)=(1-2\rho_{N})\phi(x)-O(N^{-1/2}),
and
1-2\rho_{N}
is of order
N^{-1/4}.
Denote by
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}
the supremum of
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
, and denote by
\|\phi\|_{C^{k}}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\phi^{(j)}\|_{L^{\infty}}.
Using the Taylor expansion, one verifies that the term in the square brackets of (
4.15
) is bounded above absolutely by
N^{-3/4}
multiplied by
2\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}.
For
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
let
A_{\phi}:=\sup\{|x|:x\in supp(\phi)\}
, and note that the summands in (
4.15
) vanish whenever
|N^{-1/2}x|>A_{\phi}+1
. We have thus shown that the error term satisfies a pathwise bound written in (
4.16
) just below.

Lemma 4.5
.

For all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the predictable quadratic variation of
M_{N}(\phi)
may be written as in (
4.13
), where
Q_{N}
and
\mathcal{E}_{N}
are defined by (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) respectively. Furthermore, we have uniformly over all
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
N\geq 1
the bound

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}_{N}(s,\phi)|

\displaystyle\leq 8N^{-3/4}\sigma^{2}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{[-A_{\phi}-1,A_{\phi}+1]}(N^{-1/2}x)

(4.16)

\displaystyle\leq 8N^{-1/4}\sigma^{2}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\big(Q_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{[-A_{\phi}-1,A_{\phi}+1]})-Q_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{[-A_{\phi}-1,A_{\phi}+1]})\big),

where
A_{\phi}:=\sup\{|x|:x\in supp(\phi)\}
.

What this estimate shows is that the error term
\mathcal{E}_{N}
is of the same form as
Q_{N}
, but with an extra factor of
N^{-1/4}
in front. The field
Q_{N}
will be shown to be tight in later sections, and therefore the irrelevance of
\mathcal{E}_{N}
will then be immediate from (
4.16
) without any further work needed. This error calculation illustrates a remarkable property of the model under consideration, which is that the error terms behave very nicely in relation to the original object itself, which is rare among KPZ-related models where a martingale characterization has been used, see e.g.
[
BG97
,
DT16
,
Yan22
]
where extremely careful analysis was needed to show vanishing of error terms.

Now that the important objects
M_{N}
and
Q_{N}
have been introduced, we give a brief discussion on how the proof of Theorem
1.1
will be completed. The next section is heavily devoted to studying the quadratic martingale field
Q_{N}(t,\phi)
. In Proposition
5.4
, we will establish tightness-related bounds of the form

\mathbf{E}[|Q_{N}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}(s,\phi)|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}|t-s|^{1/2}.

This will then lead to a similar regularity bound for
[M_{N}(\cdot,\phi)]
and
\mathscr{U}_{N}(\cdot,\phi)
as well.

Together with these bounds, a Kolmogorov-type lemma will tell us that the triple
(\mathscr{U}_{N},M_{N},Q_{N})
is tight. This will be the subject of Section
6
. Since the prelimiting object satisfies a lattice stochastic heat equation, one can obtain that any limit point of the triple satisfies (
4.1
) by showing that the discrete and continuous heat operators are close.

To justify why the limit point satisfies the other part (
4.2
) of the martingale problem, we study the QMF extensively in Section 5. In particular,
informally speaking, we shall show the “key estimate” that

\displaystyle Q_{N}(t,\phi)-\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathscr{U}_{N}(s,\phi)^{2}\;\;\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;\;\;\;0

if we take
N\to\infty
and then
\phi\to\delta_{a}
in that order, for any
a\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
. The precise statement is interpreted in terms of well-chosen Gaussian bump functions (see Proposition
5.3
). This type of estimate will allow us to conclude that the limit point satisfies (
4.2
).

5.
Formulas and estimates for the martingales

This section will heavily focus on obtaining crucial formulas and estimates for the quadratic martingale field (QMF)
Q_{N}
defined in (
4.14
). Later, these formulas and estimates will allow us to show the tightness of (
1.3
) and also identify the limit points. We first have two lemmas before stating the key estimate of this section.

Lemma 5.1

(Moment formulas)
.

Fix any bounded functions
\psi,\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
and
N\geq 1
. Suppose that
(R^{1},\ldots,R^{2k})
denotes the canonical process on
(\mathbb{Z}^{2k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
. Recall
\Delta_{k}(s,t)
from (
3.8
), and define
\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t):=(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{k}\cap\Delta_{k}(s,t)
. For any
\vec{s}=(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{k})\in\Delta_{k}(0,t)
, we define

(5.1)

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{N}(\vec{s})=\prod_{r\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\frac{1}{(\#\{i:s_{i}=r\})!}.

Note that
\#\{i:s_{i}=r\}=0
for all but finitely many
r\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
We have the following moment formulas.

(a)

For all
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\gamma>0
, we have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{N}(t,\psi)-\frac{\gamma}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathscr{U}_{N}(s,\phi)^{2}\bigg)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=k!N^{-k}\sum_{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}(0,t)}{\overline{g}_{N}(\vec{s})}\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu}^{(2k)}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{k}C_{N,s_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}({Ns_{i}})-N^{3/4}s_{i})}

\displaystyle\cdot C_{N,s_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}({Ns_{i}})-N^{3/4}s_{i})}\Upsilon_{N}({Ns_{i}};R^{2i-1},R^{2i})\bigg],

where

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{N}(r;X,Y)

\displaystyle:=4N^{1/2}\sigma^{2}\psi\big(N^{-1/2}(X(r)-N^{-1/4}r)\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{X(r)=Y(r)\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\gamma\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(X(r)-N^{-1/4}r)\big)\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(Y(r)-N^{-1/4}r)\big).

(b)

For all
0\leq s<t
we have the following moment formula for the increment of the QMF

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{N}(t,\psi)-Q_{N}(s,\psi)\bigg)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=(4\sigma^{2})^{k}N^{-k/2}k!\sum_{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}{\overline{g}_{N}(\vec{s})}\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu}^{(2k)}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{k}C_{N,s_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}(Ns_{i})-N^{3/4}s_{i})}^{2}

\displaystyle\psi(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}(Ns_{i})-N^{3/4}s_{i}))\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{2i-1}(Ns_{i})=R^{2i}(Ns_{i})\}}\bigg].

Proof.

Note by (
1.3
) that

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}C_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(x-N^{3/4}t)}C_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(y-N^{3/4}t)}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\cdot\mathsf{P}^{\omega}(Nt,x)\mathsf{P}^{\omega}(Nt,y)\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(x-N^{3/4}t)\big)\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(y-N^{3/4}t)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}_{(2)}^{\omega}\big[C_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(S({Nt})-N^{3/4}t)}C_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(R({Nt})-N^{3/4}t)}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(S({Nt})-N^{3/4}t)\big)\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R({Nt})-N^{3/4}t)\big)\big].

Here
\mathsf{E}_{(2)}^{\omega}
denotes quenched expectation for two independent motions
(R(r),S(r))_{r\geq 0}
in the fixed realization of the environment
\omega
. Note that if we apply
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}
to the last expression, then it is of a similar form to the definition (
4.14
) of
Q_{N}
, hence we have that

\displaystyle Q_{N}

\displaystyle(t,\psi)-\frac{\gamma}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathscr{U}_{N}(s,\phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathsf{E}_{(2)}^{\omega}[C_{N,s,N^{-1/2}(R({Ns})-N^{3/4}s)}C_{N,s,N^{-1/2}(S({Ns})-N^{3/4}s)}\Upsilon_{N}(Ns;R,S)].

From here one expands out the
k^{th}
power of both sides of this equation, and then one applies the annealed expectation over the quenched expectation to deduce (
5.2
), noting in general that by the multinomial theorem one has the following expansion for a function
f
:

\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}f(s)\bigg)^{k}=k!\sum_{0\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{k}<r}\overline{g}_{N}(\vec{s})f(s_{1})\cdots f(s_{k}).

The proof of (
5.3
) is similar. ∎

The following proposition will allow us to further analyze the moment formulas obtained in Lemma
5.1
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Assume that
\nu
is a probability measure on
[0,1]
of mean
1/2
. Fix any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\{\psi_{i},\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
be bounded continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}
.

Let
A
be a subset of
\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. Let
B=\{1,2\ldots,k\}\cap A^{c}
. Define

(5.4)

\displaystyle E_{1}(\vec{t}):=\prod_{i\in A}C_{N,t_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}(Nt_{i})-N^{3/4}t_{i})}\phi_{i}\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}(Nt_{i})-N^{3/4}t_{i})\big)

(5.5)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\prod_{i\in A}C_{N,t_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}(Nt_{i})-N^{3/4}t_{i})}\phi_{i}\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}(Nt_{i})-N^{3/4}t_{i})\big)

(5.6)

\displaystyle E_{2}(\vec{t}):=\prod_{i\in B}C_{N,t_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}({Nt_{i}})-N^{3/4}t_{i})}^{2}\psi_{i}(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}(Nt_{i})-N^{3/4}t_{i}))\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{2i-1}({Nt_{i}})=R^{2i}({Nt_{i}})\}}

Consider any uniformly bounded sequence of deterministic functions
g_{N}:\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)\to[0,\infty)
that converge uniformly to 1 on the interior of the simplex (consisting of those points
(t_{1},...,t_{k})
with all
t_{i}
distinct) as
N\to\infty
. For each
0\leq s<t\leq T<\infty
we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{-|A|-\tfrac{1}{2}|B|}(1-4\sigma^{2})^{|B|}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}_{\nu}}\bigg[\sum_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}{g_{N}(\vec{t})}E_{1}(\vec{t})E_{2}(\vec{t})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes(2k)}}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\cdot\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL_{0}^{U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}}(t_{i})\bigg],

where the expectation on the right is with respect to a
2k
-dimensional standard Brownian motion
(U^{1},\ldots,U^{2k})
, and

(5.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t}):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{2i-1\leq p<q\leq 2k}\left[L_{0}^{U^{p}-U^{q}}(t_{i})-L_{0}^{U^{p}-U^{q}}(t_{i-1})\right].

Here
\int_{0}^{t}f(s)dL_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(s)
denotes the integration of the continuous function
f:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
against the random Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure
dL_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}
induced from the increasing function
t\mapsto L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(t).

We will be most interested in the case where
g_{N}(\vec{t})=\overline{g}_{N}(\vec{t})
defined in (
5.1
).

Proof.

We are going to assume that
g_{N}\equiv 1
; the general case follows by slight modifications. Let
\nu_{N}^{*}
be the skewed sequence of measures corresponding to
\nu
, as in Definition
2.1
. For simplicity we write
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{E}_{N}
for
\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu}^{(2k)}}
and
\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu_{N}^{*}}^{(2k)}}
respectively. Let us fix any
\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)
and a subset
A
of
\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. Just as in the proof of moment convergence, the main idea of the proof is to first apply a change of measure to get rid of the divergent terms inside the expectation of
\mathbf{E}[E_{1}(\vec{t})E_{2}(\vec{t})]
and then use the weak convergence result from Theorem
3.1
. The change of measure here will be based on a glorified version of (
3.12
).

Step 1. Post-processing the expectation via tilting.

To carry out the tilting procedure, we first note that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[E_{1}(\vec{t})E_{2}(\vec{t})]=\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\widetilde{C}_{N}^{i}\Lambda_{N}^{i}\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\widetilde{C}_{N}^{1}\Lambda_{N}^{1}\mathbf{E}\left[\widetilde{C}_{N}^{2}\Lambda_{N}^{2}\cdots\mathbf{E}\left[\widetilde{C}_{N}^{k}\Lambda_{N}^{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{Nt_{k-1}}\right]\cdots\mid\mathcal{F}_{Nt_{1}}\right]\right]\!.\!

where

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N}^{i}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\phi_{i}\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}(Nt_{i})-N^{3/4}t_{i})\big)\phi_{i}\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}(Nt_{i})-N^{3/4}t_{i})\big)&i\in A\\
\displaystyle\psi_{i}(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}(Nt_{i})-N^{3/4}t_{i}))\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{2i-1}({Nt_{i}})=R^{2i}({Nt_{i}})\}}&i\in B,\end{cases}

and

(5.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{N}^{i}:=\prod_{j=2i-1}^{2k}C_{N,t_{i}-t_{i-1},N^{-1/2}(R^{j}(Nt_{i})-R^{j}(Nt_{i-1})-N^{3/4}(t_{i}-t_{i-1}))}.

We shall now apply tilting to each of the expectations on the r.h.s. of (
5.9
) and go from
\mathbf{P}
to
\mathbf{P}_{N}
. To perform the tilting for conditional expectations we need generalized versions of
\mathpzc{M}^{\lambda}(\cdot)
(defined in (
2.7
)) and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{N}
(defined in (
2.9
)):

Let us define

(5.11)

\displaystyle\mathpzc M^{[k_{1}:k_{2}],\lambda}(r)=\exp\bigg(\lambda\sum_{j=k_{1}}^{k_{2}}R^{j}(r)-\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}f_{\ell}^{\lambda,k_{2}-k_{1}+1,\nu}(R^{k_{1}}(\ell),\ldots,R^{k_{2}}(\ell))\bigg)

The above martingale is obtained in the same spirit as
\mathcal{M}^{\lambda}
by considering only the motion of

(k_{2}-k_{1}+1)
particles:
(R^{j}(\cdot))_{j=k_{1}}^{k_{2}}
.

Let us also define
\mathbf{P}_{N}
-martingales
\mathcal{G}^{1}_{N},\ldots,\mathcal{G}^{k}_{N}
by
\mathcal{G}^{i}_{N}=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{i}_{N}+\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{N},
where the
\mathbf{P}_{N}
-martingales
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{N}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{i}_{N}
are defined by
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{i}_{N}(0)=\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{N}(0)=0
, and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{i}_{N}(r+1)-\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{i}_{N}(r)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle N^{-1/4}\sum_{j=2i-1}^{2k}(R^{j}(r+1)-R^{j}(r)-(2\mu_{\nu_{N}^{*}}-1)),&Nt_{i}\leq r<Nt_{i+1}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{N}(r+1)-\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{N}(r)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{q=1}^{v}\big(\log(m_{b_{q},n_{q}-b_{q}})-\mathbf{E}_{N}[\log(m_{b_{q},n_{q}-b_{q}})|\mathcal{F}_{r}]\big),&Nt_{i}\leq r<Nt_{i+1}\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
m_{b,n-b}
is defined in (
2.12
) and the parameters
v=v(i,r),n_{q}=n_{q}(i,r),
and
b_{q}=b_{q}(i,r)
are obtained deterministically from the path
(R^{2i-1},\ldots,R^{2k})
as follows. Assume that at time
r
, the
2k-2i+2
“particles” of
(R^{2i-1}(r),\ldots,R^{2k}(r))\in\mathbb{Z}^{2k-2i+2}
may be grouped into
v=v(i,r)
disjoint groups with all particles in each distinct group at the same site in
\mathbb{Z}
. Assume that the
v
respective groups contain
n_{1},\ldots,n_{v}
respective particles, where
n_{q}=n_{q}(i,r)
are positive integers such that
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{v}=2k-2i
. From time
r
to
r+1
in the path
(R^{2i-1},\ldots,R^{2k})
, assume (for each
1\leq q\leq v
) in the
q^{th}
group, that
b_{q}=b_{q}(i,r)
of the
n_{j}
particles go up one step, so that
n_{j}-b_{j}
particles go down one step.

The processes
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}
are martingales of the same form as those in the proof of Proposition
2.5
, but where only the last
2k-2i+2
of the
2k
particles are taken into account on the time interval
[Nt_{i},Nt_{i+1})\cap\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, by the exact same argument as in that proof, each of the martingales
\mathcal{G}^{i}_{N}
satisfies the bound (
3.2
) for some absolute constant
C>0
independent of
N
. Define
W_{N}^{i}(r)=\sum_{s\leq r}\log\mathbf{E}_{N}[e^{\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}(s)-\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}(s-1)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-1}]
. Set
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}}:=\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}-W_{N}^{i}
. We will now see that the exponentials of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}}
are the “correct” martingales to tilt the expressions in the lemma statement in such a way that Theorem
3.1
is applicable.

Just like (
3.12
), one can check that for each
i
the conditional expectation
\mathbf{E}[\mathbf{F}\mid\mathcal{F}_{Nt_{i}}]
can be tilted as follows:

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[\mathbf{F}\mid\mathcal{F}_{Nt_{i}}]=\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[\frac{\mathpzc M^{[2i-1:2k]}(Nt_{i-1})}{\mathpzc M^{[2i-1:2k]}(Nt_{i})}\exp\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}}(Nt_{i})\right)\mathbf{F}\mid\mathcal{F}_{Nt_{i}}\right],

where
\mathbf{F}
is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\{(R^{2i-1}(r),\ldots,R^{2k}(r))_{r\in[0,Nt]}\})
.

Using this, we tilt each of the conditional expectations in (
5.9
) to get

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[E_{1}(\vec{t})E_{2}(\vec{t})\right]=\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\mathpzc M^{[2i-1:2k]}(Nt_{i-1})}{\mathpzc M^{[2i-1:2k]}(Nt_{i})}\exp\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}}(Nt_{i})\right)\widetilde{C}_{N}^{i}{\Lambda}_{N}^{i}\right]

Step 2. Convergence.
We shall now study the weak convergence of the sum of random variables inside the expectation on the r.h.s. of (
5.13
).
We use the notations of rescaled processes from (
3.1
). Recalling the identity (
3.14
) and the bound in (
3.15
) we see that

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\mathpzc M^{[2i-1:2k],N^{-1/4}}(Nt_{i-1})}{\mathpzc M^{[2i-1:2k],N^{-1/4}}(Nt_{i})}\widetilde{C}_{N}^{i}=\exp\left(4\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{2i-1<p<q\leq 2k}[\mathscr{V}_{N}^{pq}(t_{i})-\mathscr{V}_{N}^{pq}(t_{i-1})]-\widetilde{\mathsf{Err}}_{N}(\vec{t})\right).

where

(5.14)

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\mathsf{Err}}_{N}(\vec{t})|\leq\mathrm{C}\left(\mathscr{T}_{N}(t)+N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq p<q\leq 2k}\mathscr{V}_{N}^{pq}(t)\right).

Let us define

W_{N}^{\vec{t}}(s):=W_{N}^{1}(Ns)+\cdots+W_{N}^{k}(Ns)
and
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{\vec{t}}(s):=\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}(Ns)+\cdots+\mathcal{G}_{N}^{k}(Ns)

for
s\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and linearly interpolated for
s\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
. Let us view
W_{N}
and
\mathcal{G}_{N}
as random continuous functions from
\Delta_{k}(0,t)\times[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
. Using the same arguments from the proof of Theorem
3.1
, it follows that
W_{N}
and
\{\mathcal{G}_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight in the space of
C(\Delta_{k}(0,t)\times[0,t])
equipped with uniform topology, and furthermore
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{\vec{t}}
satisfies (
3.2
) with constant
C
independent of
\vec{t}
. By Theorem
3.1
and Corollary
3.2
we know that any limit point as
N\to\infty
of the sequence

(5.15)

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathcal{G}_{N}^{\vec{t}},W_{N}^{\vec{t}},\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathscr{V}_{N},\mathscr{T}_{N},N^{-|B|/2}\hskip-17.07182pt\sum_{u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{|B|}\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{i\in B}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}_{N}^{2i-1}(u_{i})=\mathbf{X}_{N}^{2i}(u_{i})\}}\delta_{(u_{1},\ldots,u_{|B|})}\bigg)

(considered under the measure
\mathbf{E}_{N}
) is of the form

(5.16)

\bigg(\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}},\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}}\rangle,\mathbf{U},(L^{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq 2k},\mathbf{0},(1-4\sigma^{2})^{-|B|}\prod_{i\in B}dL^{2i-1,2i}(t_{i})\bigg),

where
\mathbf{U}
is a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
L^{i,j}
are its pairwise local times, and
\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}}
are martingales satisfying (
3.5
). Here the last coordinate has a topology of
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{k}(0,T))
which was defined in the statement of Corollary
3.2
, while all other coordinates have a uniform topology with respect to all relevant variables. Notice that the map from
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{k}(0,T))\times C(\Delta_{k}(0,T))\to\mathbb{R}
given by
(f,\mu)\mapsto\int_{\Delta_{k}(0,T)}f\;d\mu
is a continuous map. Using the continuous mapping theorem, we therefore see that for the limit point (
5.16
) of (
5.15
) one has

\displaystyle N^{-|A|-\tfrac{1}{2}|B|}(1-4\sigma^{2})^{|B|}\sum_{\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\mathpzc M^{[2i-1:2k]}(Nt_{i-1})}{\mathpzc M^{[2i-1:2k]}(Nt_{i})}\exp\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}_{N}^{i}}(Nt_{i})\right)\widetilde{C}_{N}^{i}{\Lambda}_{N}^{i}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}}\rangle(t)+\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\cdot\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL^{2i-1,2i}(t_{i}),

where we again emphasize that the objects in the prelimit are viewed as observables under
\mathbf{E}_{N}
. Thanks to the estimates in Lemma
2.7
, Proposition
2.8
, and (
3.10
), we see that the prelimiting sum above is uniformly integrable. Now we finally prove the proposition. Taking into account the identity in (
5.13
), the preceding observations imply that for every subsequence of indices
N\to\infty
, there is a further subsequence along which we have

\displaystyle N^{-|A|-\tfrac{1}{2}|B|}(1-4\sigma^{2})^{|B|}\sum_{\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}[E_{1}(\vec{t})E_{2}(\vec{t})]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}\left[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}}\rangle(t)+\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\cdot\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL^{2i-1,2i}(t_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}[e^{\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}^{\vec{t}}\rangle(t)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}(\mathbf{U})]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\cdot\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL^{2i-1,2i}(t_{i})\bigg].

Here
\mathbf{E}_{\text{lim}}
denotes a possible limit point on the canonical space, of the entire tuple of processes given by (
5.15
), and we are viewing (
5.16
) as the canonical process on that space. The inner conditional expectation in the last expression equals
1
due to (
3.5
). Thus the last expression must be equal to the right side of (
5.7
), completing the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.3

(Key estimate for the QMF)
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}
let
\xi(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x^{2}}
, and let
\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\xi(\varepsilon^{-1}(x-a))
.
Then for all
t>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{N}(t,\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a})-\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathscr{U}_{N}(s,\xi_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}^{a})^{2}\bigg)^{2}\bigg]=0.

Furthermore, we have the bound

(5.18)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon>0\\
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left[1\wedge\frac{1}{|\log a|^{2}}\right]\!\cdot\!\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{N}(t,\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a})-\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathscr{U}_{N}(s,\xi_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}^{a})^{2}\bigg)^{2}\bigg]<\infty.

The above proposition is the key estimate that will allow us to identify limit points. This proposition also illustrates why we cannot hope to obtain convergence in a space of continuous functions. Indeed if this were possible, then by first equality in the definition (
4.14
) of the field
Q_{N}
it is easy to see that in (
5.17
), the correct coefficient would have to be
8\sigma^{2}=2\cdot 4\sigma^{2}
rather than
\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}
(where the extra factor of 2 comes from the fact that
Z_{N}^{\omega}(t,x)
as defined in (
4.5
) are nonzero only at those points such that
t-x
is even). Clearly, both coefficients cannot be correct unless
\mathscr{U}_{N}
vanishes in the limit which is certainly not the case, as we have already shown that its moments of all orders converge to a nontrivial limit.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
5.1

(a)
with
\gamma=\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}
, and Proposition
5.2
with
k=2
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{N}(t,\psi)-\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathscr{U}_{N}(s,\phi)^{2}\bigg)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\bigg(\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\bigg)^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 4}}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\psi(X_{s_{i}}^{i})\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\phi(X^{i})\phi(Y^{i})ds_{i}\right)\right]

(5.19)

\displaystyle=2\bigg(\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\bigg)^{2}\hskip-2.84544pt\!\cdot\!\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 4}}\left[\int\limits_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\hskip-8.5359pte^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\psi\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}+Y_{s_{i}}^{i})\big)\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\phi(X^{i})\phi(Y^{i})ds_{i}\right)\hskip-2.84544pt\right]

for all
\psi,\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is defined in (
5.8
), and
(X^{1},X^{2},Y^{1},Y^{2})
is a 4d standard BM under
\mathbf{P}_{B^{\otimes 4}}
.
The second equality in the above equation follows by observing that
X_{u}^{i}=Y_{u}^{i}
for
u
in the support of
L_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(du)
.

We shall now write
\mathbf{E}
instead of
\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 4}}
for convenience. Let us now take

\psi(x):=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\varepsilon^{2}}}e^{-(x-a)^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}},\quad\phi(x):=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon^{2}}}e^{-(x-a)^{2}/2\varepsilon^{2}},

in (
5.19
). Using the identity
\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(x)\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(y)=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon}((x+y)/2)\xi^{0}_{2\varepsilon}(x-y)
, we may now write (
5.19
) as

(5.20)

\displaystyle 2\bigg(\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\bigg)^{2}\hskip-2.84544pt\!\cdot\!\mathbf{E}\left[\int\limits_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\hskip-5.69046pte^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}+Y_{s_{i}}^{i})\big)\!\cdot\!(\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}-Y_{s_{i}}^{i})ds_{i})\right)\hskip-2.84544pt\right]\!.\!

Let us write
U^{i,-}:=X^{i}-Y^{i}
and
U^{i,+}:=X^{i}+Y^{i}
. Note that under
\mathbf{P}_{B^{\otimes 4}}
the four processes
U^{1,-},U^{1,+},U^{2,-},U^{2,+}
are independent Brownian motions with diffusion coefficient
2
. This enables us to view (
5.20
) as

\displaystyle 2\bigg(\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\bigg)^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{i}}^{i,+})\big)\cdot(\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{i,-}}(s_{i})-\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{i}}^{i,-})ds_{i})\right)\right]

(5.21)

\displaystyle=:2\bigg(\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\bigg)^{2}[A_{1}(\varepsilon)-A_{2}(\varepsilon)-A_{3}(\varepsilon)+A_{4}(\varepsilon)],

where

\displaystyle A_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\,\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{1,-}}(s_{1})\,\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{2,-}}(s_{2})\right]

\displaystyle A_{2}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{2}}^{2,-})\,\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{1,-}}(s_{1})\,ds_{2}\right]

\displaystyle A_{3}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{1}}^{1,-})\,\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{2,-}}(s_{2})\,ds_{1}\right]

\displaystyle A_{4}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{1}}^{1,-})\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{2}}^{2,-})\,ds_{1}\,ds_{2}\right].

From here, the goal is to show that (
5.21
) vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
as long as
a\neq 0
, as well as establish a bound given by the right side of (
5.18
) for each of the four terms
A_{i}(\varepsilon)
.

Note that informally
\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{i,\pm}}(s_{i})
may be written as
\delta_{0}(U^{i,\pm})ds_{i}
which suggests that each of the
A_{i}
may be written in terms of Brownian bridge expectations. Indeed this is the case, and consequently the proofs of the desired convergence statements and bounds for the terms
A_{i}(\varepsilon)
rely purely on elementary (albeit lengthy) disintegration formulas for Brownian motion at its endpoint, and these proofs can be copied verbatim from
[
DDP24
, Proof of Proposition 5.3: Steps 2 and 3]
, replacing the coefficient
\sigma
appearing there with our coefficient
\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}
throughout the proof. For brevity, we do not reproduce the details here.
∎

With the “key estimate” proved, next we focus on obtaining bounds that will be useful for proving the tightness of the rescaled field (
1.3
).

Proposition 5.4

(Estimates for moments of the increments of QMF)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
T>0
. Then there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(k,T)>0
such that for all bounded measurable functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
and all
0\leq s<t\leq T
with
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
one has that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\big[(Q_{N}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}(s,\phi))^{k}\big]\leq\mathrm{C}\|\phi\|^{k}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}(t-s)^{k/2}.

Furthermore fix
p>1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(p,\varepsilon,k,T)>0
such that for all functions
\phi\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
and all
\varepsilon\leq s<t\leq T
one has

(5.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[(Q_{N}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}(s,\phi))^{k}\big]\leq\mathrm{C}\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}^{k}(t-s)^{k}.

Proof.

We are going to use the same notation and the same family of martingales from the proof of Proposition
5.2
. Using (
5.3
) together with the trivial bound
|\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{j}(Nt_{j})-N^{3/4}t_{j}))|\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}},
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\big(Q_{N}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}(s,\phi)\big)^{k}]

\displaystyle\leq N^{-k/2}k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\sum_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}_{\nu}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{N,t_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j}({Nt_{j}})-N^{3/4}t_{j})}^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{2j-1}(Nt_{j})=R^{2j}(Nt_{j})\}}\bigg]

(5.24)

\displaystyle=k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\cdot N^{-k/2}\sum_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}_{\nu_{N}^{*}}}\bigg[\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{2j-1}(Nt_{j})=R^{2j}(Nt_{j})\}}\bigg],

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})

\displaystyle:=\exp\bigg(\mathcal{G}_{N}^{\vec{t}}(t)-W_{N}^{\vec{t}}(t)\bigg),

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(4\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{2i-1<p<q\leq 2k}[\mathscr{V}_{N}^{pq}(t_{i})-\mathscr{V}_{N}^{pq}(t_{i-1})]-\widetilde{\mathsf{Err}}_{N}(\vec{t})\right),

and where all of these objects are exactly the same as those introduced in the proof of Proposition
5.2
. The equality in (
5.24
) is due to the argument given in the proof of Proposition
5.2
. As before, let us abbreviate
\mathbf{E}_{N}:=\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}_{\nu_{N}^{*}}}.
Recalling the measure
\gamma_{N}
introduced in (
3.9
), let us rewrite (
5.24
) as

k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{N}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})\gamma_{N}(d\vec{t})\bigg].

Now using a trivial bound

\int_{\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})\gamma_{N}(d\vec{t})\leq\|\mathbf{A}_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))}\|\mathbf{B}_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))}\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)),

we have by Holder’s inequality that

(5.25)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{N}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t)}\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})\gamma_{N}(d\vec{t})\bigg]

(5.26)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[\|\mathbf{A}_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))}^{3}\big]^{1/3}\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[\|\mathbf{B}_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))}^{3}\big]^{1/3}\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))^{3}\big]^{1/3}.

Recalling
\mathscr{V}^{ij}
from Theorem (
3.1
), note that
\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))\leq\big(\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(t)-\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(s)\big)^{k}.
By Lemma
2.7
, we can bound
\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))^{3}\big]^{1/3}\leq C(t-s)^{k/2}
where
C
does not depend on
N
. This is because of the fact that
\mu_{\nu_{N}^{*}}(1-\mu_{\nu_{N}^{*}})-\sigma_{\nu_{N}^{*}}^{2}
is positive for large enough
N
. Thus it suffices to bound
I_{1},I_{2}
independently of
N
.

Note that the martingales
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{\vec{t}}(t)
satisfy (
3.2
), by their construction in the proof of Proposition
5.2
. Consequently an application of Doob’s
L^{p}
inequality combined with Proposition
2.8
allows us to bound the expected maximum
\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[\|\mathbf{A}_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))}^{3}\big]^{1/3}
independently of
N
. Finally using Lemma
2.6
we can bound
\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[\|\mathbf{B}_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Delta_{k}^{N}(s,t))}^{3}\big]^{1/3}
for large enough
N
as well (as again
\mu_{\nu_{N}^{*}}(1-\mu_{\nu_{N}^{*}})-\sigma_{\nu_{N}^{*}}^{2}
is positive for large enough
N
). This verifies the bound in (
5.22
).

For (
5.23
). appealing to the moment formula for the increment of QMF from (
5.3
) and the convergence from (
5.7
) we have

(5.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big(Q_{N}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}(s,\phi)\big)^{k}\bigg]

(5.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=k!\bigg(\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\bigg)^{k}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 2k}}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(U_{t_{j}}^{2j})dL_{0}^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(t_{j})\bigg],

where
\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})
is defined in (
5.8
). The rest of the proof is analogous to the proof of
[
DDP24
, Eq. (5.43)]
.
∎

Recall the martingale field
M_{N}
from (
4.9
). The next estimate will obtain a
L^{p}
bound on its optional quadratic variation from (
4.11
), which will be the main tool in obtaining tightness for the field (
1.3
).

Proposition 5.5

(Optional quadratic variation bound)
.

Fix
p\geq 1
. For all
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we have that

(5.29)

\mathbb{E}\big[\big|[M_{N}(\phi)]_{t}-[M_{N}(\phi)]_{s}\big|^{p}\big]^{1/p}\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}|t-s|^{1/2}.

Here
C
is independent of
N,\phi,s,t
.

Proof.

The main idea of the proof will be to split the quadratic variation
[M_{N}(\phi)]
into a “predictable part” (denoted
A_{N}
below) which is easy to control, and a “discontinuity part” (denoted
B_{N}
below) which we expect to vanish as
N\to\infty
. Then we bound each of these separately.

It suffices to prove the claim when
p=2k
for some positive integer
k
. Letting
\eta_{N}(r,x):=1-2\omega_{r,x+N^{-1/4}r}
, recall from (
4.10
) that for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:

M_{N}(t,\phi)=\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}x\big)Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)\eta_{N}(r,x).

where
(\nabla_{N}\phi)(x):=(1-\rho_{N})\phi(x-N^{-1/2})-\rho_{N}\phi(x+N^{-1/2}).
A first-order Taylor expansion of
\phi
yields that
\|\nabla_{N}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}N^{-1/4}
. Then for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have that

\displaystyle(M_{N}

\displaystyle(t+N^{-1},\phi)-M_{N}(t,\phi))^{2}=A_{N}(Nt,\phi)+B_{N}(Nt,\phi).

where

\displaystyle A_{N}(r,\phi)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}x\big)^{2}Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)^{2}\eta_{N}(r,x)^{2},

\displaystyle B_{N}(r,\phi)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}x\big)(\nabla_{N}\phi)\big(N^{-1/2}y\big)Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,y)\eta_{N}(r,x)\eta_{N}(r,y).

We will separately obtain bounds of the desired form for both
A_{N}
and
B_{N}
. Using the fact that
\eta_{N}(r,x)^{2}\leq 1
deterministically, and using the definition (
4.14
) of
Q_{N}
, we obtain the pathwise bound

\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}A_{N}(r,\phi)\leq\sigma^{-2}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{2}\big(Q_{N}(t,\mathbf{1})-Q_{N}(s,\mathbf{1})\big).

In particular, by (
5.22
) we see that

(5.30)

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}A_{N}(r,\phi)\bigg)^{2k}\bigg]\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{4}\mathbb{E}\big[\big(Q_{N}(t,\mathbf{1})-Q_{N}(s,\mathbf{1})\big)^{2k}\big]\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{2k}|t-s|^{k}.

Now let us bound the moments of
B_{N}
.
Note that

\displaystyle(B_{N}(r,\phi))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{4}\bigg(\sum_{x}Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x)\bigg)^{4}=N^{-1}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{4}\cdot\left(\mathsf{E}^{\omega}\big[C_{N,N^{-1}r,N^{-1/2}(R(r)-N^{-1/4}r)}\big]\right)^{4}.

Taking the annealed expectation of a product of
B_{N}
’s we get

(5.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}B_{N}(r_{j},\phi)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq N^{-k}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{4k}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\mathsf{E}^{\omega}\big[C_{N,N^{-1}r_{j},N^{-1/2}(R(r_{j})-N^{-1/4}r_{j})}\big]\right)^{4}\right].

By Hölder’s inequality the right-hand side of the above equation is less than

(5.32)

\displaystyle N^{-k}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{4k}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu}^{(4k)}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{4k}C_{N,N^{-1}r_{j},N^{-1/2}(R_{i}(r_{j})-N^{-1/4}r_{j})}\bigg]\right)^{1/k}.

Using the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
5.4
, the above expectation can be bounded uniformly over
r_{j}
’s in
[0,Nt]
.
Using the fact that
\mathbb{E}[B_{N}(r,\phi)|\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{r-1}]=0,
we then obtain from Burkholder-Davis-Gundy that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}

\displaystyle B_{N}(r,\phi)\bigg)^{2k}\bigg]\leq C_{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}B_{N}(r,\phi)^{2}\bigg)^{k}\bigg]

\displaystyle=C_{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{r_{1},...,r_{k}=Ns}^{Nt}B_{N}(r_{1},\phi)^{2}\cdots B_{N}(r_{k},\phi)^{2}\bigg]\leq C_{k}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{4k}|t-s|^{k}.

This gives the desired claim after combining this bound with the other bound (
5.30
) for
A_{N}(r,\phi)
.
∎

6.
Tightness and identification of limit points

Throughout this section, we are going to fix a terminal time
T>0
. To prove Theorem
1.1
we need to introduce a number of preliminaries.

Definition 6.1
.

Recall the lattice
\Lambda_{N}
from (
4.3
) and define its rescaled version

\Psi_{N,T}:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(Nt,N^{1/2}x)\in\Lambda_{N},\;\;\;0\leq t\leq T\}.

Definition 6.2
.

For
(s,y)\in\Lambda_{N}
define
p_{N}(s,y)
to be the transition density at time
s
and position
y
of a random walker on
\Lambda_{N}
with increment distribution given by going from
0
to
-1+N^{-1/4}
with probability
\rho_{N}
(as defined in (
2.1
)), and from
0
to
1+N^{-1/4}
with probability
1-\rho_{N}
.

Define linear operators
D_{N},L_{N},K_{N}
on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by

\displaystyle D_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle:=N\big[f(t+N^{-1},x)-f(t,x)\big],

\displaystyle L_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle:=N\big[f(t+N^{-1},x-N^{-3/4})-\big(\rho_{N}f(t,x-N^{-1/2})+(1-\rho_{N})f(t,x+N^{-1/2})\big)\big],

\displaystyle K_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle:=N^{-1}\sum_{(s,y)\in\Psi_{N,T+1}}p_{N}(Ns,N^{1/2}y)f(t-s,x-y).

These equalities should be understood by integration against smooth functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

The sum defining
K_{N}f
is well defined because it is actually a finite sum, as
p_{N}
is finitely supported for bounded time intervals. Intuitively it is clear that
L_{N}
is a diffusively rescaled version of the discrete heat operator
\mathcal{L}_{N}
from Section
4
, but which acts on tempered distributions rather than functions on
\Lambda_{N}
. Indeed if
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
then a second-order Taylor expansion shows that
L_{N}\varphi
converges in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as
N\to\infty
to
(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f
, i.e.,
L_{N}
approaches the continuum heat operator. Then
K_{N}
is the inverse operator to
L_{N}
, in the following sense.

Lemma 6.3
.

L_{N}K_{N}f=K_{N}L_{N}f=f
whenever
f
is a tempered distribution supported on
[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}
with
b-a<T+1
.

Proof.

Note that for each
N
the operators
K_{N},L_{N}
are continuous on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, since they are finite linear combinations of translation operators. Therefore it suffices to prove the claim for all smooth functions
f
that have compact support contained in
(a,b)\times\mathbb{R}
, since these are dense in the subset of distributions supported on
[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}
, with respect to the topology of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The smooth claim is true by direct calculations since
p_{N}(s,y)
is the kernel for the inverse operator to the discrete heat operator
\mathcal{L}_{N}
introduced in Section
4
.
∎

Definition 6.4
.

With the fields
M_{N}
and
Q_{N}
as defined in (
4.9
) and (
4.14
) respectively, we will associate random elements of
C([0,T+1],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
by the formulas
\widehat{M}_{N}(t,\phi)=0
for
t\in[0,N^{-1}]
and

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{N}(t,\phi)

\displaystyle:=M_{N}(t-N^{-1},\phi)-M_{N}(0,\phi),\;\;\;\;\;t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{N}(t,\phi)

\displaystyle:=Q_{N}(t,\phi),\;\;\;\;\;t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. We define these fields by linear interpolation for
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

For any
T
, note that
C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
embeds naturally into the linear subspace of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
consisting of distributions supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, thus we can make sense of
D_{N}f,L_{N}f,K_{N}f
for all
f\in C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})),
and these will be elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in general.

Definition 6.5
.

We will say that two tempered distributions
f,g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
agree on
[0,T]
if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
(f,\varphi)=(g,\varphi)
for all
\varphi
supported on
[-\varepsilon,T+\varepsilon]\times\mathbb{R}.

Definition 6.6
.

Sample the environment
\omega
and then define a collection of coefficients
a_{N}(s,y)
with
(s,y)\in\Lambda_{N}
as
a_{N}(0,y)=\mathbf{1}_{\{y=0\}}
and for
s\geq 1
,

a_{N}(s,y)=\omega_{0,0}p_{N}(s-1,y-1-N^{-1/4})+(1-\omega_{0,0})p_{N}(s-1,y+1-N^{-1/4}).

Then define the distribution
\mathfrak{p}_{N}\in C([0,T+1],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by

\mathfrak{p}_{N}(t,\cdot):=N^{-1}\sum_{y:(t,y)\in\Psi_{N,T+1}}a_{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}y)\delta_{y}

and linearly interpolated for
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Although its definition is tedious,
\mathfrak{p}_{N}
admits the following simple description: it agrees with the density field
\mathscr{U}_{N}
from (
1.3
) on the time interval
[0,N^{-1}]
, and it is a linearly interpolated solution of the discrete heat equation
\mathcal{L}_{N}a_{N}=0
at all positive times in
N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. The purpose of this will be to make certain technical details work later. Since
\mathfrak{p}_{N}
behaves deterministically after time
N^{-1}
, its scaling limit will simply be the continuum heat kernel.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\widehat{M}_{N}
be as defined above. Furthermore, let
\mathscr{U}_{N}
be as defined in (
1.3
). Restrict
\mathscr{U}_{N}
to
[0,T]
thus viewed as an element of
C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
. Then
L_{N}(\mathscr{U}_{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})
agrees with
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
on
[0,T]
. Consequently
\mathscr{U}_{N}
agrees with
\mathfrak{p}_{N}+K_{N}D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
on
[0,T]
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathscr{U}_{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N}
and
\widehat{M}_{N}
both vanish on
[0,N^{-1}]
, consequently
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}(0,\phi)=L_{N}(\mathscr{U}_{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})(0,\phi)=0
for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. On the other hand,
L_{N}\mathfrak{p}_{N}(t,\cdot)=0
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
t>0
, consequently
L_{N}(\mathscr{U}_{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})(t,\phi)=L_{N}\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\phi)
for such
t
.

Recall
v_{N}
defined in (
4.6
). Then it is clear from (
4.9
) that

D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}(t,\phi)=N\big[M_{N}(t,\phi)-M_{N}(t-N^{-1},\phi)\big]=\bigg(\sum_{x:(t,x)\in\Psi_{N,T}}Nv_{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}x)\delta_{x}\;\;,\;\;\phi\bigg),

for all
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
.
Now, with
Z^{\omega}_{N}
defined in (
4.5
) we have that for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\cdot)=\sum_{x:(t,x)\in\Psi_{N,T}}Z^{\omega}_{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}x)\delta_{x}.

With
\mathcal{L}_{N}
as defined in Section
4
, it is then clear that for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the expression for
L_{N}\mathscr{U}_{N}(t,\cdot)
can be written as

\displaystyle N\sum_{x:(t,x)\in\Psi_{N,T}}(\mathcal{L}_{N}Z^{\omega}_{N})(Nt,N^{1/2}x)\delta_{x}=N\sum_{x:(t,x)\in\Psi_{N,T}}v_{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}x)\delta_{x},

which is the same as the expression for
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}(t,\phi).
Since linear operators respect linear interpolation, and since all of the fields
\mathscr{U}_{N},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N},\mathfrak{p}_{N}
are defined via linear interpolation, we have
L_{N}(\mathscr{U}_{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})=D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
for
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
as well.

Finally, if we view the restriction of
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
to
[0,T]
as an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, then we can apply
K_{N}
to both sides and we obtain that
(\mathscr{U}_{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})(t,\cdot)=K_{N}D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}(t,\cdot)
for all
t\in[0,T]
by Lemma
6.3
.
∎

6.1.
Weighted Hölder spaces and Schauder estimates

We now introduce various natural topologies for our field
\mathscr{U}_{N}
and its limit points. We then discuss how the heat flow affects these topologies and record Kolmogorov-type lemmas that will be key in showing tightness under these topologies. We begin by recalling many familiar and useful spaces of continuous and differentiable functions that have natural metric structures.
For
d\geq 1
, we denote by
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of all compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define its
C^{r}
norm as

(6.1)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{r}}:=\sum_{\vec{\alpha}}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D^{\vec{\alpha}}f(\mathbf{x})|

where the sum is over all
\vec{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
\sum\alpha_{i}\leq r
and
D^{\vec{\alpha}}:=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{d}}^{\alpha_{d}}
denotes the mixed partial derivative.
