We now recall the definition of weighted Hölder spaces from
[
HL15
, Definitions 2.2 and 2.3]
. For the remainder of this paper, we shall work with
elliptic and parabolic
weighted Hölder spaces with polynomial weight function

(6.2)

\displaystyle w(x):=(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

for some fixed
\tau>1
. We introduce these weights because weighted spaces will be more convenient to obtain tightness estimates. Since the solution of (
1.4
) started from Dirac initial condition is known to be globally bounded away from
(0,0)
, we expect that it is possible to remove the weights throughout this section, but this would require more precise moment estimates than the ones we derived in previous sections, which take into account spatial decay of the fields.

Definition 6.8

(Elliptic Hölder spaces)
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{|f(x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{|x-y|\leq 1}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{w(x)|x-y|^{\alpha}}.

For
\alpha<0
we let
r=-\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
and we define
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
to be the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\phi\in B_{r}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators
S^{\lambda}_{x}
are defined by

(6.3)

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi(y)=\lambda^{-1}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 6.9

(Function spaces)
.

Let
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
be as in Definition
6.8
. We define
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
to be the space of continuous maps
g:[0,T]\to C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}),
equipped with the norm

\|g\|_{C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|g(t)\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}.

Here and henceforth we will define
\Psi_{[a,b]}:=[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}
and we will define
\Psi_{T}:=\Psi_{[0,T]}.

So far we have used
\phi,\psi
for test functions on
\mathbb{R}
. To make the distinction clear between test functions on
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we shall use variant Greek letters such as
\varphi,\vartheta,\varrho
for test functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In some instances, we will explicitly write
(f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
or
(g,\phi)_{\mathbb{R}}
when we want to be clear about the space in which we are pairing.

Definition 6.10

(Parabolic Hölder spaces)
.

We define
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
to be the set of functions on
\Psi_{T}
that are restrictions to
\Psi_{T}
of some function in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(in particular we do not impose that elements of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
vanish at the boundaries of
\Psi_{T}).

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\frac{|f(t,x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{|s-t|^{1/2}+|x-y|\leq 1}\frac{|f(t,x)-f(s,y)|}{w(x)(|t-s|^{1/2}+|x-y|)^{\alpha}}.

For
\alpha<0
we let
r=-\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
and we define
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
to be the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Psi_{T})}}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators are defined by

(6.4)

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi(s,y)=\lambda^{-3}\varphi(\lambda^{-2}(t-s),\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

An important property of both the parabolic and elliptic spaces is that one has a continuous embedding
C^{\alpha,\tau}\hookrightarrow C^{\beta,\tau}
whenever
\beta<\alpha.
In fact this embedding is compact, though we will not use this. We also have the following embedding of function spaces inside parabolic spaces.

Lemma 6.11
.

For
\alpha<0,\tau>0
one has a continuous embedding
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\hookrightarrow C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
given by identifying
v=(v(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
with the tempered space-time distribution given by

(v,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=\int_{0}^{T}(v(t),\varphi(t,\cdot))_{\mathbb{R}}dt.

The proof is straightforward from the definitions and is omitted.

Remark 6.12

(Derivatives of distributions)
.

Let
\alpha<0
. By definition, any element
f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
admits an
L^{2}
-pairing with any smooth function
\varphi:\Psi_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay, which we can write as
(f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}.
Consequently there is a natural embedding
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
which is defined by formally setting
f
to be zero outside of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
. More rigorously, this means that the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-pairing of
f
with any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is defined to be equal to
(f,\varphi|_{\Psi_{T}})_{\Psi_{T}}
.

The image of this embedding consists of some specific collection of tempered distributions that are necessarily supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}.
Consequently we can sensibly define
\partial_{t}f
and
\partial_{x}f
as elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
whenever
f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
. Explicitly these derivatives are defined by the formulas

(\partial_{t}f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}:=-(f,\partial_{t}\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(\partial_{x}f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}:=-(f,\partial_{x}\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}

for all smooth
\varphi:\Psi_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay. This convention on derivatives will be useful for certain computations later. From the definitions, when
\alpha<0
one can check that for such
f
one necessarily has
\partial_{t}f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
and
\partial_{x}f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-1,\tau}(\Psi_{T}).

The latter statement fails for
\alpha>0
. Indeed, by our convention of derivatives,
\partial_{t}f
may no longer be a smooth function (or even a function) even if
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
. This is because such an
f
gets extended to all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by setting it to be zero outside
\Psi_{T}
. In particular, if
f
does not vanish on the boundary of
\Psi_{T}
, then it may become a discontinuous function under our convention of extension to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Due to these discontinuities, the distributional derivative
\partial_{t}f
can be a tempered distribution with singular parts along the boundaries (one may verify that
\partial_{t}f
can be at best an element of
C^{-2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
for generic
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
). In our later computations, we never take derivatives of functions in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
with
\alpha>0
.

We now discuss the smoothing effect of heat flow on these weighted Hölder spaces.

Proposition 6.13

(Smoothing effect on elliptic spaces)
.

For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
t>0
define

\mathpzc P_{t}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p(t,x-y)f(y)dy.

Then for all
\alpha\leq\beta<1
, there exists
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha,\beta,T)>0
such that

\|\mathpzc P_{t}f\|_{C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\mathrm{C}t^{-(\beta-\alpha)/2}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}

uniformly over
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}
and
t\in[0,T]
. In particular,
\mathpzc P_{t}
extends to a globally defined linear operator on
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
which maps boundedly into
C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}).

A proof may be found in
[
HL15
, Lemma 2.8]
in the case of an exponential weight. The proof for polynomial weights is identical.

For the parabolic Hölder spaces, the following lemma states that the heat flow improves the regularity by a factor of
2
and provides a Schauder-type estimate.

Proposition 6.14

(Schauder estimate)
.

For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
let us define

(6.5)

\displaystyle Kf(t,x):=\int_{\Psi_{T}}p(t-s,x-y)f(s,y)dsdy,

where
p
is the standard heat kernel for
t>0,x\in\mathbb{R}
and
p(t,x):=0
for
t<0.
Then for all
\alpha<-1
with
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}
there exists
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha)>0
independent of
f
such that

\|Kf\|_{C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}.

In particular
K
extends to a globally defined linear operator on
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
that maps boundedly into
C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T}).
Furthermore, if
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
, then
K(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f=f
.

We remark that the last statement is only true because of our convention on distributional derivatives that we have explained in Remark
6.12
. Without that convention, that statement would be false even for a smooth function
f
that does not vanish on the line
\{t=0\}
.

Proof.

See
[
DDP24
, Proposition 6.6]
.
∎

Corollary 6.15
.

Define
J:C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
by

Jf(t,x):=\big(f,p(t,x-\cdot)\big)_{\mathbb{R}},

J
is a bounded linear operator for any
\alpha<1
and any
\tau>0.
Consider the operator
\widehat{\mathpzc P}_{t}:=J\mathpzc P_{t}
with
\mathpzc P_{t}
defined in Proposition
6.13
. By the semigroup property of the heat kernel, we have that

(6.6)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathpzc P}_{s}f(t,x)=(Jf)(t+s,x).

Furthermore, the operators satisfy

(6.7)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathpzc P}_{t}f\|_{C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot t^{-(\beta-\alpha)/2}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})},

where
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha,\beta,T)>0
is independent of
t
and
f
.

Proof.

The first part follows by using the Schauder estimate in Proposition
6.14
, noting that
Jf=K(\delta_{0}\otimes f)
where for
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
the latter distribution is defined by
(\delta_{0}\otimes f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:=(f,\varphi(0,\cdot))_{\mathbb{R}}.
Directly from the definitions one can check that
f\mapsto\delta_{0}\otimes f
is bounded from
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T}).
Finally, (
6.7
) follows from Proposition
6.13
.
∎

We next define a space-time distribution that is supported on a single temporal cross-section:

Definition 6.16
.

Let
\alpha<0
. Given some
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
and
b\in[0,T]
we define
\delta_{b}\otimes f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
by the formula

(\delta_{b}\otimes f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:=(f,\varphi(b,\cdot))_{\mathbb{R}}.

Directly from the definitions of the scaling operators in (
6.3
) and (
6.4
), it is clear that for fixed
b\in[0,T]
, the linear map
f\mapsto\delta_{b}\otimes f
is bounded from
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
as long as
\alpha<0.
It is also clear that
\delta_{b}\otimes f
is necessarily supported on the line
\{b\}\times\mathbb{R}
. The following lemma shows that under mild conditions,
\delta_{T}\otimes f
vanishes upon the action of the heat flow
K
defined in (
6.5
).

We end this subsection by recording a Kolmogorov-type lemma for the function spaces and parabolic Hölder spaces introduced at the beginning of this subsection. It will be crucial in proving tightness in those respective spaces.

Lemma 6.17

(Kolmogorov lemma)
.

Let
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be the space of all random variables defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with finite second moment. We have the following:

(a)

(Function space)
Let
(t,\phi)\mapsto V(t,\phi)
be a map from
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
which is linear and continuous in
\phi
. Fix a non-negative integer
r
. Assume there exists some
\kappa>0,p>1/\kappa
and
\alpha<-r
and
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\kappa,\alpha,p,{T})>0
such that one has

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[|V(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{p}]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\lambda^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[|V(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-V(s,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{p}]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\lambda^{\alpha-\kappa}|t-s|^{\kappa},

uniformly over all smooth functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}
with
\|\phi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
, and uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
0\leq s,t\leq T
. Then for any
\tau>1
and any
\beta<\alpha-\kappa
there exists a random variable
\big(\mathscr{V}(t)\big)_{t\in[0,T]}
taking values in
C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
such that
(\mathscr{V}(t),\phi)=V(t,\phi)
almost surely for all
\phi
and
t
. Furthermore, one has that

\mathbf{E}[\|\mathscr{V}\|^{p}_{C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}]\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime},

where
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
depends on the choice of
\alpha,\beta,p,\kappa,
and the constant
\mathrm{C}
appearing in the moment bound above but not on
V,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}
.

(b)

(Parabolic Hölder Space) Let
\varphi\mapsto V(\varphi)
be a bounded linear map from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Assume
V(\varphi)=0
for all
\varphi
with support contained in the complement of
\Psi_{T}
. Recall
S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}
from (
6.4
). Fix a non-negative integer
r
. Assume there exists some
p>1
and
\alpha<0
and
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha,p)>0
such that one has

\mathbf{E}[|V(S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq\mathrm{C}\lambda^{\alpha},

uniformly over all smooth functions
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|\varphi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
, and uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}
. Then for any
\tau>1
and any
\beta<\alpha-3/p
there exists a random variable
\mathscr{V}
taking values in
C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
such that
(\mathscr{V},\varphi)=V(\varphi)
almost surely for all
\varphi.
Furthermore one has that

\mathbf{E}[\|\mathscr{V}\|^{p}_{C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}]\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime},

where
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
depends on the choice of
\alpha,p,
and the constant
\mathrm{C}
appearing in the moment bound above but not on
V,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}
.

A proof of the above results may be adapted from the proof of Lemma 9 in Section 5 of
[
MW17
]
. We remark that we do not actually need uniformity over a large class of test functions as we have written above, just a single well-chosen test function would suffice (e.g. the Littlewood-Paley blocks as used in
[
MW17
]
or the Daubechies wavelets in
[
HL15
]
).

6.2.
Tightness

Here we will prove tightness of the rescaled field from (
1.3
).

Lemma 6.18
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>0
. Let
K_{N},D_{N}
be the operators from Definition
6.2
. Recall the function spaces from Definition
6.9
, and let
\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\alpha,\tau}
denote the closed linear subspace of
C([0,T+1],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
consisting of those paths
v=(v(t))_{t\in[0,T+1]}
such that
v(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,N^{-1}]
. Then we have the operator norm bound

\sup_{N\geq 1}\|K_{N}D_{N}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\alpha,\tau}\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}<\infty.

Proof.

Note that
v(t)=0
for
t\in[0,N^{-1}]
ensures that
D_{N}v
is actually a continuous path taking values in
C([0,T+1-N^{-1}],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
, consequently so is
K_{N}D_{N}v
. Let us now define four families of linear operators on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, indexed by
N
. For
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and
s\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
let

\displaystyle P_{N}(s)f(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{y\in N^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}-N^{3/4}s}p_{N}(Ns,N^{1/2}y)f(x-y),

\displaystyle\delta_{N}f(x)

\displaystyle:=N(P_{N}(N^{-1})-I)f(x)=N\big[\rho_{N}f(x+N^{-\frac{1}{2}}-N^{-\frac{3}{4}})+(1-\rho_{N})f(x-N^{-\frac{1}{2}}-N^{-\frac{3}{4}})-f(x)\big]

\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{*}f(x)

\displaystyle:=N\big[\rho_{N}f(x-N^{-1/2}+N^{-3/4})+(1-\rho_{N})f(x+N^{-1/2}+N^{-3/4})-f(x)\big],

\displaystyle P^{*}_{N}(s)f(x)

\displaystyle:=\big(I+N^{-1}\delta_{N}^{*}\big)^{Ns}f(x)=\sum_{y\in N^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}-N^{3/4}s}p_{N}(Ns,N^{1/2}y)f(x+y).

Then for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
each of the four expressions
P_{N}(s)f,\delta_{N}f,\delta_{N}^{*}f,P_{N}^{*}(s)f
also make sense as elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
. Note that
\delta_{N}
and
\delta^{*}_{N}
, both of which approximate the spatial Laplacian
\partial_{x}^{2}
, are adjoint to each other on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore
P_{N}
and
P^{*}_{N}
are also adjoint to each other. Now let
v=(v(t))_{t\in[0,T+1]}\in\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\alpha,\tau}
. For
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in[0,T]
, if we apply summation by parts and use the fact that
v
vanishes on
[0,N^{-1}]
we can write

\displaystyle\big(K_{N}D_{N}v(t),\phi\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(N^{-1}\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}NP_{N}(t-s)\big[v(s+N^{-1})-v(s)\big]\;\;,\;\;\phi\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(v(t+N^{-1})-0+\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\big[P_{N}(t-s)-P_{N}(t-s-N^{-1})\big](v(s))\;\;,\;\;\phi\bigg)

\displaystyle=(v(t+N^{-1}),\phi)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}(\delta_{N}P_{N}(t-s-N^{-1})v(s),\phi)

\displaystyle=(v(t+N^{-1}),\phi)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}(v(s),P_{N}^{*}(t-s-N^{-1})\delta^{*}_{N}\phi).

Here we are using the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-pairing between distributions and smooth functions. By the definition of the function spaces (Definition
6.9
), we must replace
\phi
by
S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi
(where the scaling operators are given in Definition
6.8
) in the last expression and then study the growth as
\lambda
becomes close to 0.

The first term on the right side is completely straightforward to deal with: the growth is at worst
\lambda^{\alpha}(1+x^{2})^{\tau}
uniformly over
\lambda,\phi,x,T
, since we assumed
v\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).
To deal with the second term, we claim that one has

(6.8)

|(v(s),P_{N}^{*}(t-s-N^{-1})\delta_{N}^{*}S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|\lesssim\big(\lambda^{\alpha-2}\wedge(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\big)(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

uniformly over
s<t\in[0,T]
, as well as
\lambda,\phi,x,N
and
v
with
\|v\|_{C([0,T+1],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}\leq 1
. Indeed the bound of the form
\lambda^{\alpha-2}
follows by noting that when
t-s
is much smaller than
\lambda
,
P_{N}^{*}(t-s)
is essentially the identity operator, so we can effectively disregard the heat kernel and note that
\delta_{N}^{*}S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi
paired with
v(s)
satisfies a bound of order
\lambda^{\alpha-2}
, uniformly over
N
by e.g. second-order Taylor expansion. Likewise the bound of the form
(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}
is obtained by noting that when
\lambda
is very small compared to
t-s
,
P_{N}^{*}(t-s-N^{-1})\delta^{*}_{N}S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi
behaves like
S_{x}^{\sqrt{t-s}}\phi
, giving a bound of order
(\sqrt{t-s})^{\alpha-2}
after applying
\delta^{*}_{N}
and pairing with
v(s)
. This proves the bound (
6.8
).

Now the fact that
\|K_{N}D_{N}v\|
can be controlled by
\|v\|
follows simply by noting that uniformly over
0<\lambda^{2}\leq t\leq T
one has

\int_{0}^{t}\big(\lambda^{\alpha-2}\wedge(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\big)ds=\int_{0}^{t-\lambda^{2}}(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}ds+\int_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{t}\lambda^{\alpha-2}ds\leq\mathrm{C}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda^{\alpha}.

This implies the uniform bound on the operator norm of
K_{N}D_{N}
.
∎

Proposition 6.19

(Tightness of all relevant processes)
.

The following are true.

(1)

The fields
\widehat{M}_{N}
from Definition
6.4
may be realized as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\alpha<-3
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(2)

The fields
\mathscr{U}_{N}
from (
1.3
) may be realized as an element of as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\alpha<-3
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(3)

The fields
\widehat{Q}_{N}
from Definition
6.4
may be realized as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\gamma<-1
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(4)

Let
\alpha<-3,\gamma<-1,
and
\tau>1
. Let
(M^{\infty},Q^{\infty},U^{\infty})
be a joint limit point of
(\widehat{M}_{N},\widehat{Q}_{N},\mathscr{U}_{N})
in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).
For all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the process
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\in[0,T]}
is a continuous martingale with respect to the canonical filtration on that space, and moreover

(6.9)

\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2}).

Proof.

Take any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1.
Recall
S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}
from (
6.4
). Using the first bound in Proposition
5.4
, we have

\mathbb{E}[(Q_{N}(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-Q_{N}(s,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi))^{k}]\leq\mathrm{C}|t-s|^{k/2}\lambda^{-k},

uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in(0,1],0\leq s,t\leq T
with
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since
Q_{N}(0,\phi)=0
by definition, the assumptions of Lemma
6.17

(a)
are therefore satisfied for any
\kappa\leq 1/4
, any
p>1/\kappa
, and any
\alpha\leq-1
, and we conclude the desired tightness for
\widehat{Q}_{N}=Q_{N}
. This proves Item
(3)
.

Now we address the tightness of the
\widehat{M}_{N}
. Using the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality and then Proposition
5.5
, we have that

(6.10)

\mathbb{E}[(M_{N}(t,\phi)-M_{N}(s,\phi))^{8}]^{1/8}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\big([M_{N}(\phi)]_{t}-[M_{N}(\phi)]_{s})\big)^{4}\big]^{1/8}\leq\mathrm{C}(t-s)^{1/4}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R})},

where
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(k)>0
is free of
\phi,s,t,N.

This gives

\mathbb{E}[(M_{N}(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-M_{N}(s,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi))^{8}]^{1/8}\leq\mathrm{C}(t-s)^{1/4}\lambda^{-2}

uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in(0,1],0\leq s,t\leq T
, and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}\leq 1
with support contained in the unit interval. Moreover
\widehat{M}_{N}(0,\phi)=0
by definition, therefore the assumptions of Lemma
6.17

(a)
are satisfied with
\kappa=1/4
,
p=8>1/\kappa
, and any
\alpha\leq-2
. Hence, we conclude the desired tightness for
\widehat{M}_{N}
. This proves Item
(1)
.

Now tightness for the fields
\mathscr{U}_{N}
is immediate from Lemma
6.18
, since we know from Lemma
6.7
that
\mathscr{U}_{N}=\mathfrak{p}_{N}+K_{N}D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
where we view
K_{N}D_{N}
as a bounded operator from
\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\alpha,\tau}\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
and the convergence of
\mathfrak{p}_{N}
in this topology is straightforward to deal with. This proves Item
(2).

We next show that the limit point
M^{\infty}(\phi)
is a martingale indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since
M_{N}(0,\phi)=0
, from (
6.10
), we see that
\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}[M_{N}(t,\phi)^{2k}]<\infty.
Thus
M^{\infty}(\phi)
is a martingale since martingality is preserved by limit points under the uniform integrability assumption. Continuity is guaranteed by the definition of the spaces in which we proved tightness. In the prelimit we know from (
4.13
) that

M_{N}(t,\phi)^{2}-\big((2\rho_{N}-1)^{2}\sqrt{N}\big)Q_{N}(t,\phi^{2})-\mathcal{E}_{N}(t,\phi)

is a martingale indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where the error term
\mathcal{E}_{N}
is defined in (
4.15
) and satisfies the bound (
4.16
). Note that
\big((2\rho_{N}-1)^{2}\sqrt{N}\big)\to 1
as
N\to\infty
. By (
4.16
) and tightness estimates (
5.22
) of
Q_{N}
, it follows that
\mathcal{E}_{N}(t,\phi)
vanishes in probability in the topology of
C[0,T]
, so we conclude (again by uniform
L^{p}
boundedness guaranteed by Proposition
5.4
) that
M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)^{2}-Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2})

is a martingale. This verifies (
6.9
) completing the proof of Item
(4)
.
∎

Lemma 6.20

(Controlling the difference between the discrete and continuum heat operators)
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>1
. Let
K_{N}
be as in Definition
6.2
, and let
K
be as in (
6.5
). Then we have the operator norm bound

\|K_{N}-K\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha-1,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq CN^{-1/4}.

Here
C
is independent of
N
.

The above bound is crude, we do not claim optimality of the Holder exponents here.

Proof.

Define
f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x):=(f,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Psi_{T})}
. It suffices to show that

\sup_{\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq 1}\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\sup_{\lambda\in[(0,1]}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\frac{|(K_{N}-K)f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x)|}{(1+x^{2})^{\tau}\lambda^{\alpha-1}}\leq CN^{-1/4},

where the scaling operators are defined by

S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi(s,y)=\lambda^{-3}\varphi(\lambda^{-2}(t-s),\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We shall use a probabilistic interpretation of the kernels to prove this. Note that

(K_{N}-K)f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x)=\frac{1}{N}\hskip-5.69046pt\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\hskip-5.69046pt\mathbf{E}[f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,x-W_{N}(s))]-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{E}[f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,x-W(s))]ds,

where
W_{N}(s)=N^{-1/2}R_{N}(Ns)
for the discrete-time random walk
(R_{N}(r))_{r\geq 0}
with increment distribution described in Definition
6.2
, and
W
is a standard Brownian motion. Define
W_{N}(s)
by linear interpolation for
s\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

By the KMT coupling
[
KMT76
]
, we may assume that
W_{N}
and
W
are all coupled onto the same probability space so that
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}|W_{N}(s)-W(s)|\leq GN^{-1/4}
where
G
is a random variable on that same probability space independent of
N
such that
\mathbf{E}|G|^{p}<\infty
for all
p
. Note that
-1/4
is the optimal exponent here because, despite KMT giving a better exponent in principle, the increments of the original random walk
R_{N}
are not centered but rather they have a non-zero mean of order
N^{-3/4}
. Consequently, by the definition of the parabolic spaces, we have uniformly over
s,t\in[0,T]
the bound

\displaystyle|f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,x-W_{N}(s))-f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,x-W(s))|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sup_{u\in\big[-|x|-|W(s)|-|W_{N}(s)|,\;|x|+|W(s)|+|W_{N}(s)|\big]}|\partial_{x}f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,u)|\bigg)\cdot|W_{N}(s)-W(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\lambda^{\alpha-1}(1+4x^{2}+4W(s)^{2}+4(W_{N}(s))^{2})^{\tau}\bigg)\cdot GN^{-1/4}.

Here the factor
\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\lambda^{\alpha-1}
can be deduced using e.g. Remark
6.12
which says that
\partial_{x}
boundedly reduces the parabolic regularity by 1 exponent. We also used the fact that
(|x|+|y|+|z|)^{2}\leq 4x^{2}+4y^{2}+4z^{2}.

We claim that

\mathbf{E}\big|G\cdot(1+4x^{2}+4W(s)^{2}+4(W_{N}(s))^{2})^{\tau}\big|<C(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

for a constant
C=C(\alpha,T,\tau)
independent of
N,x,s.
To prove this, one uses
(a+b+c+d)^{\tau}\leq 4^{\tau}(a^{\tau}+b^{\tau}+c^{\tau}+d^{\tau})
, then one notes that
\sup_{N}\mathbf{E}|G\cdot\sup_{s\leq T}(|W(s)|^{2\tau}+|W_{N}(s)|^{2\tau})|<\infty
by e.g. Cauchy-Schwartz.

Consequently the above expression for
(K_{N}-K)f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x)
can be bounded in absolute value by a universal constant times
\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\lambda^{\alpha-1}N^{-1/4}
. ∎

Lemma 6.21

(Controlling the difference between the discrete and continuum time-derivatives)
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>1
. The derivative operator
\partial_{s}:C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
which was defined in Remark
6.12
, is a bounded linear map. Furthermore, let
D_{N}
be as in Definition
6.2
. Then we have the operator norm bounds

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\|D_{N}\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})}

\displaystyle<\infty.

\displaystyle\|D_{N}-\partial_{s}\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha-4,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{-1}.

Proof.

The proof that
\partial_{s}
is bounded is straightforward from the definitions of the spaces. Note that
(D_{N}f,\varphi)=(f,D_{N}^{-}\varphi)
where
D_{N}^{-}\varphi(t,x)=N\big[\varphi(t,x)-\varphi(t-N^{-1},x)\big]
. Recall
S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}
from (
6.4
). From the definition of the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
one verifies directly that if
v\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
with norm less than or equal to 1, then for
N>\lambda^{-1}
one has

\int_{0}^{T}|v_{t}\big(D_{N}^{-}(S^{\lambda}_{(s,y)}\varphi)(t,\cdot)\big)|dt\leq(1+y^{2})^{\tau}\int_{s-\lambda^{2}}^{s+\lambda^{2}}\lambda^{\alpha-4}dt\leq(1+y^{2})^{\tau}\lambda^{\alpha-2}

uniformly over
(s,y)\in\Psi_{T}
and
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|\varphi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
where
r=\lceil-\alpha\rceil.
Here the bound of order
\lambda^{\alpha-4}
may be deduced by writing
D_{N}^{-}\varphi(t,x)=N\int_{t-N^{-1}}^{t}\partial_{s}\varphi(s,y)ds
, interchanging the two integrals, and noting that
\partial_{s}(S^{\lambda}_{(s,y)}\varphi)(t,\cdot)
satisfy such a bound. This proves the first operator norm bound.

Now we prove the second bound. Define
f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x):=(f,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Psi_{T})}
. It suffices to show that

\sup_{\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq 1}\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\frac{|(D_{N}-\partial_{s})f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x)|}{(1+x^{2})^{\tau}\lambda^{\alpha-4}}\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{-1},

where the scaling operators are defined by

S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi(s,y)=\lambda^{-3}\varphi(\lambda^{-2}(t-s),\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Note that

\displaystyle|(D_{N}-\partial_{s})f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|N\big(f^{\lambda}_{\varphi}(t+N^{-1},x)-f^{\lambda}_{\varphi}(t,x)\big)-\partial_{s}f^{\lambda}_{\varphi}(t,x)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|N\int_{t}^{t+N^{-1}}\big(\partial_{s}f^{\lambda}_{\varphi}(u,x)-\partial_{s}f^{\lambda}_{\varphi}(t,x)\big)du\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq N\int_{t}^{t+N^{-1}}\|\partial_{s}^{2}f^{\lambda}_{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}([t,t+N^{-1}]\times\{x\})}|t-u|du\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{-1}\|\partial_{s}^{2}f^{\lambda}_{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}([t,t+N^{-1}]\times\{x\})}.

From the definitions one has
\|\partial_{s}^{2}f^{\lambda}_{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}([t,t+N^{-1}]\times\{x\})}\leq(1+x^{2})^{\tau}\lambda^{\alpha-4}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}.

∎

Since we expect to obtain a Dirac initial data in the limiting SPDE, there is an additional singularity at the origin that we have not yet taken into account. To fix this issue, we now formulate a tightness result taking into account this singularity, by starting the field (
1.3
) from some positive time
\varepsilon
(which should be thought of as being close to
0
).

Proposition 6.22

(Regularity of limit points)
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0,
and consider the fields
\mathscr{U}_{N,\varepsilon}(t,\cdot):=\mathscr{U}_{N}(t+\varepsilon,\cdot),
for
t\geq 0
. Set
\mathscr{U}_{N,\varepsilon}(t,\cdot):=0
for
t<0
. The fields
\mathscr{U}_{N,\varepsilon}
may be realized as random variables taking values in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\tau>1
and
\alpha<-3
. Furthermore, they are tight with respect to that topology, and any limit point is necessarily supported on
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
for any
\kappa>0
.

Proof.

We already know from Item (2) of Proposition
6.19
that the
\mathscr{U}_{N}
are tight in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
. We also know from Item (1) of Proposition
6.19
that
\widehat{M}_{N}
are tight in
C([0,T+1],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
, which is embeds continuously into
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
by Lemma
6.11
. Thus using the first bound in Lemma
6.21
, we have that
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
are tight in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2}(\Psi_{T})
.

Consider any joint limit point
(U^{\infty},\mathscr{M}^{\infty})
as
N\to\infty
of the pair
(\mathscr{U}_{N},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N})
in the product space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\times C^{\alpha-2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
. By Lemmas
6.7
and
6.20
we may conclude that

(6.11)

U^{\infty}=p+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty},

where
p(t,x)=(2\pi t)^{-1/2}e^{-x^{2}/2t}\mathbf{1}_{\{t\geq 0\}}

is the (deterministic) standard heat kernel, and
K
is the operator defined in (
6.5
).

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Now we consider the
\varepsilon
-translates of these statements. It is automatic from Item (2) of Proposition
6.19
that
\mathscr{U}_{N,\varepsilon}
are tight in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
. Likewise, it is automatic from Item (1) that the family
\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}:=\widehat{M}_{N}(\varepsilon+\cdot)
is tight in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
so that from Lemma
6.21
we see that
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}
are tight in
C^{\alpha-2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T}).
Let us now take a joint limit point
(U^{\infty},\mathscr{M}^{\infty},U^{\infty,\varepsilon},\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon})
of the family
(\mathscr{U}_{N},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N},\mathscr{U}_{N,\varepsilon},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}).
On one hand we necessarily have
U^{\infty,\varepsilon}(t)=U^{\infty}(t+\varepsilon)
and on the other hand we necessarily have that
U^{\infty}=p+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty}.
From the last statement in Proposition
6.14
, this then forces the relation

\mathscr{U}^{\infty,\varepsilon}=K(\delta_{0}\otimes U^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot))+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon},

where the tensor product was defined in Definition
6.16
. Now we notice
K(\delta_{0}\otimes U^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot))=J\big(U^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot)\big)
, where
J
is defined in Corollary
6.15
. We thus have the Duhamel equation

(6.12)

\mathscr{U}^{\infty,\varepsilon}=J\big(U^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot)\big)+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}.

For technical reasons that will be made clear below, we now replace
\varepsilon
by
\varepsilon/2
and
T
by
T+1
. We now claim that for all
q>0

(6.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}\|_{C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa-3/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T+1})}^{q}]<\infty.

Let us now complete the proof of regularity assuming (
6.13
).

Using (
6.13
) and Proposition
6.14
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big\|K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}\big\|_{C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T+1})}^{q}\bigg]<\infty.

This implies that the restriction of
K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
.

•

Let
h:=\mathscr{U}_{N}(\varepsilon/2,\cdot)
. By Proposition
6.19
Item (2) we have
h\in C^{-2-\kappa,\tau}
almost surely. So from (
6.7
) with
\alpha:=-2-\kappa
and
\beta:=\frac{1}{2}-\kappa,
it follows that
\widehat{\mathpzc P}_{\varepsilon/2}h\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T+1})
almost surely.
Since from (
6.6
), we know
Jh(t+\varepsilon/2,x)=\widehat{\mathpzc P}_{\varepsilon/2}h(t,x)
, we thus have that the restriction of
Jh
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
.

Thanks to the above two bullet points and the relation (
6.12
), we have showed that the restriction of
U^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
. This is equivalent to the fact that
U^{\infty,\varepsilon}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
. This completes the proof modulo (
6.13
).

Proof of (
6.13
)
. We shall show (
6.13
) with
\varepsilon/2
and
T+1
replaced by
\varepsilon
and
T
respectively. For
\alpha<0
, the derivative operator
\overline{\partial}_{s}:C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
which is defined by sending
(v_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
to the distribution

(6.14)

(\partial_{s}v,\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Psi_{T})}:=v_{T}(\varphi(T,\cdot))-\int_{0}^{T}v_{t}(\partial_{t}\varphi(t,\cdot))dt-v_{0}(\varphi(0,\cdot)),

whenever
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T}),
is a bounded linear map. Indeed this operator is just the composition of the embedding map of Lemma
6.11
with the
\partial_{s}
operator which we proved was bounded in Lemma
6.21
.

Let
\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}:=\widehat{M}_{N}(\varepsilon+\cdot)-\widehat{M}_{N}(\varepsilon)
and
\widehat{Q}_{N,\varepsilon}:=\widehat{Q}_{N}(\varepsilon+\cdot)-\widehat{Q}_{N}(\varepsilon)
. Let us consider any joint limit point
(Q^{\infty,\varepsilon},M^{\infty,\varepsilon},\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon})
of
(\widehat{Q}_{N,\varepsilon},\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}
) in the space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\times C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\times C^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T}),
where
\gamma<-1
. Then one may verify from the second bound in Lemma
6.21
that one necessarily has
\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}=\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty,\varepsilon}
. Furthermore by Proposition
6.19
one has martingality of
M_{t}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\phi)
, with
\langle M^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\phi)\rangle=Q^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\phi^{2})
.

Let us consider any smooth function
\varphi
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}}\leq 1.
Recall
S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}
from (
6.4
), and notice that
\big(\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi\big)^{2}\leq\lambda^{-10}\mathbf{1}_{[t-\lambda^{2},t+\lambda^{2}]\times[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}
. We will now estimate the
q^{th}
moments of
\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)=\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)
. By making
\varepsilon
smaller and
T
larger we may simply ignore the boundary terms in (
6.14
). Note that for fixed
s,t,x,\lambda
the martingale
u\mapsto M_{u}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi(s,\cdot))
is constant outside of the interval
[t-\lambda^{2},t+\lambda^{2}]
. Thus we may apply the inequalities of Minkowski and then Burkholder-Davis-Gundy in (
6.14
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[|\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{t+\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[\big|M_{s}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi(s,\cdot))\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}ds

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}_{q}\int_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{t+\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{t+\lambda^{2}}^{\infty,\varepsilon}\big((\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)^{2}(s,\cdot)\big)-Q_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{\infty,\varepsilon}\big((\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)^{2}(s,\cdot)\big)\bigg)^{q/2}\bigg]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathrm{C}_{q}\lambda^{-3}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{t+\lambda^{2}}^{\infty,\varepsilon}\big(\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}\big)-Q_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{\infty,\varepsilon}\big(\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}\big)\bigg)^{q/2}\bigg]^{1/q}

where
\mathrm{C}_{q}>0
.
For any
\delta>0
, by the second bound in Proposition
5.4
we find that the last expression may be bounded above by

2\mathrm{C}\lambda^{-3}(2\lambda^{2})^{1/2}\|\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}\|_{L^{1+\delta}(\mathbb{R})}^{1/2}=\mathrm{C}\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\big[\frac{\delta}{2(1+\delta)}\big]}.

Given any
\kappa>0
, by making
\delta=\delta(\kappa)
close to
0
and making
q=q(\kappa)
large enough, we may then apply Lemma
6.17
(b)
to conclude (
6.13
).
∎

6.3.
Identification of the limit points

In this subsection, we identify the limit points as the solution of the stochastic heat equation (
1.4
) and thereby prove our weak convergence theorem: Theorem
1.1
.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
\mu
be a random variable in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
such that for some smooth even (deterministic)
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and some
\delta>0
one has

\sup_{a,\varepsilon}(1\wedge a^{1-\delta})\mathbb{E}[(\mu,\phi^{a}_{\varepsilon})^{2}]<\infty

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}[(\mu,\phi_{\varepsilon}^{a})^{2}]=0,\text{ for all }a\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\},

where
\phi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\phi(\varepsilon^{-1}(x-a)).
Then
(\mu,\psi)=0
almost surely for all
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Define
\mu^{\varepsilon}(x):=\mu*\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)
so that
(\mu,\phi^{a}_{\varepsilon})=\mu^{\varepsilon}(a).
Given some smooth
\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
of compact support note that
(\mu^{\varepsilon},\psi)\to(\mu,\psi)
a.s. as
\varepsilon\to 0
. This is a purely deterministic statement. Thus it suffices to show that
(\mu^{\varepsilon},\psi)\to 0
in probability. To prove that, suppose that the support of
\psi
is contained in
[-S,S]
and note by Cauchy-Schwarz that

|(\mu^{\varepsilon},\psi)|=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu^{\varepsilon}(a)\psi(a)da\bigg|\leq\bigg[\int_{[-S,S]}\mu^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}da\bigg]^{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

so that by taking expectation and applying Jensen we find

\mathbb{E}[|(\mu^{\varepsilon},\psi)|]\leq\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\bigg[\int_{[-S,S]}\mathbb{E}[\mu^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}]da\bigg]^{1/2}.

Since by assumption
\mathbb{E}[\mu^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}]\leq Ca^{\delta-1}
and
\mathbb{E}[\mu^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}]\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
the dominated convergence theorem now gives the result by letting
\varepsilon\to 0
on the right side. This proves the claim for
\psi
of compact support. For general
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
we may find a sequence
\psi_{n}\to\psi
in the topology of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, with each
\psi_{n}
compactly supported. Then
0=(\mu,\psi_{n})\to(\mu,\psi)
a.s. as
n\to\infty
.
∎

Theorem 6.24

(Solving the martingale problem)
.

Consider the triple of processes
(\mathscr{U}_{N},M_{N},Q_{N})_{t\geq 0}
, where
\mathscr{U}_{N}
,
M_{N}
, and
Q_{N}
are defined in (
1.3
), (
4.9
), and (
4.14
) respectively. Fix
\alpha<-3,\gamma<-1,
and
\tau>1.
These triples are jointly tight in the space

C\big([0,T]\;,\;C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\big).

Consider any joint limit point
(U^{\infty},M^{\infty},Q^{\infty})
. Then for any
s>0
, the process
(t,x)\mapsto U_{s+t}^{\infty}(x)
is necessarily supported on the space
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
. Furthermore,
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\geq 0}
is a continuous martingale for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, and moreover for all
0<s\leq t<T
one has the almost sure identities

(6.15)

\displaystyle M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-M_{s}^{\infty}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(U_{t}^{\infty}(x)-U_{s}^{\infty}(x))\phi(x)dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}U_{u}^{\infty}(x)\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)dxdu

(6.16)

\displaystyle\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2})

(6.17)

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-Q_{s}^{\infty}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{s}^{t}(U_{u}^{\infty}(x))^{2}\phi(x)\,du\,dx.

Before going into the proof, we remark that with some inspection it may be verified that all quantities make sense given the spaces they lie in, as long as we choose
\kappa<1/2
to ensure that
U^{\infty}
is a continuous function in space-time away from
t=0
.

Proof.

Most parts of the following theorem are already established in previous propositions and lemmas. The tightness of the triple was shown in Proposition
6.19
. Consider any limit point
(U^{\infty},M^{\infty},Q^{\infty}).
The fact that for any
s>0
, the process
(t,x)\mapsto U^{\infty}(s+t,x)
is necessarily supported on the space
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
was proved in Proposition
6.22
. From Proposition
6.19
we have that for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the process
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale. (
6.16
) is already proven in Proposition
6.19
as (
6.9
). All we are left to show is (
6.15
) and (
6.17
).

Proof of (
6.15
).

In (
6.11
) we obtained that
U^{\infty}=p+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty}
where (just as we observed after (
6.14
)) one necessarily has
\mathscr{M}^{\infty}=\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty}
. By disregarding the boundary terms implies that

(\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty},\varphi)=((\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})U^{\infty},\varphi)

for all
\varphi
of compact support contained in
[\varepsilon,T-\varepsilon]\times\mathbb{R}
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Since both
M^{\infty}
and
U^{\infty}
lie in spaces with strong enough topologies, taking
\varphi(u,x)
to approach the function
(u,x)\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{[s,t]}(u)\phi(x)
in the above equation leads to (
6.15
).

Proof of (
6.17
).
Fix any
0\leq t\leq T
and let
\mu
be a
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
valued random variable defined as

(6.18)

\displaystyle(\mu,\phi):=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{t}(U_{s}^{\infty}(x))^{2}\phi(x)\,ds\,dx.

We claim that
(\mu,\phi)=0
a.s. for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).
This will validate (
6.17
). To verify this, let us define
\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\xi(\varepsilon^{-1}(x-a))
with
\xi(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x^{2}}
. Using the “key estimate” (
5.17
) of Proposition
5.3
, one verifies that the assumptions of Lemma
6.23
hold for
\mu
with this family of mollifiers.

To see why Lemma
6.23
is applicable, first note that for fixed
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the difference between the sum appearing in (
5.17
) and integral over
[0,t]
of the same quantity tends to zero in probability with respect to the topology of
C[0,T]
, since we proved tightness of
\mathscr{U}_{N}
in a topology given by the norm of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
. Consequently that sum in (
5.17
) converges in law, jointly with
\mathscr{U}_{N}
, to the integral appearing in (
6.18
). Then an application of (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) shows that
(\mu,\phi)
as defined by (
6.18
) satisfies the conditions of Lemma
6.23
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, since (
5.18
) gives a polylogarithmic bound which is less than
\mathrm{C}a^{-\delta}
for arbitrary
\delta>0
. This is enough to complete the proof.
∎

We now complete the proof of our main theorem, Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We continue with the notation and setup of Theorem
6.24
. We have already established the tightness of
\mathscr{U}_{N}
in Proposition
6.19
. Consider any limit point
U^{\infty}
of
\mathscr{U}_{N}
. From the previous theorem, we already know that
(t,x)\mapsto U_{t+\varepsilon}^{\infty}(x)
is a continuous function in space and time. From the three equations (
6.15
), (
6.16
), and (
6.17
) in Theorem
6.24
it follows that the martingale problem for (
1.4
) is satisfied by any limit point
U^{\infty}
. We refer the reader to
[
BG97
, Proposition 4.11]
for the characterization of the law of (
1.4
) as the solution to this martingale problem.

The result there is only stated for continuous initial conditions, so what this really shows is that for any
\varepsilon>0
the law of the continuous field
(t,x)\mapsto U^{\infty}(t+\varepsilon,x)
is that of the solution of (
1.4
) with initial condition
U^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot).
Thus we still need to pin down the initial data as
\delta_{0}
, by showing that we can let
\varepsilon\to 0
and see that the limit of
U^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot)
is equal to
\delta_{0}
in some sense.

In
[
Par18
, Section 6]
there is a general approach to do exactly this. Specifically, it suffices to show as in Lemma 6.6 of that paper the two bounds

(6.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|U_{t}^{\infty}(x)|^{r}]^{2/r}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot t^{-1/2}p(t,x),

(6.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|U_{t}^{\infty}(x)-p(t,x)|^{r}]^{2/r}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot p(t,x),

where
r>1
is arbitrary,
p
is the standard heat kernel, and
\mathrm{C}
is independent of
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Clearly, it suffices to show this when
r
is an even integer. The solution of (
1.4
) with
\delta_{0}
initial condition certainly satisfies this bound, and by the moment convergence result in Section
3.1
, we know any limit point
U^{\infty}
must satisfy
\mathbb{E}[(\int_{\mathbb{R}}U_{t}^{\infty}(x)\phi(x)dx)^{k}]=\mathbf{E}[(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)\phi(x)dx)^{k}]
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, where
(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)
solves (
1.4
) with
\delta_{0}
initial data. From here we can conclude by letting
\phi\to\delta_{x}
that
\mathbb{E}[U_{t}^{\infty}(x)^{k}]=\mathbf{E}[\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)^{k}]
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Consequently, we may immediately deduce (
6.19
) and (
6.20
) by the corresponding bounds for
\mathcal{U}_{t}
. This completes the proof.
∎

6.4.
Creation of independent noise in the limit

In this subsection, we prove our independent noise result: Theorem
1.4
.

Definition 6.25
.

Define for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the field

(6.21)

\mathpzc W_{N}(t,\phi):=N^{-3/4}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)\eta_{N}(r,x)

where
\eta_{N}({r,x}):=2\omega_{r,x+rN^{-1/4}}-1,
whenever
r-(x+rN^{-1/4})
is even, and
\eta_{N}({r,x})=0
whenever
r-(x+rN^{-1/4})
is odd. We also define
\mathpzc W_{N}(t,\phi)
by linear interpolation whenever
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Recall the linear operator
\partial_{s}
from Remark
6.12
, which was shown to be bounded in Lemma
6.21
. We prove the following version of Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem 6.26
.

Fix
\alpha<-3,\tau>1
. Then the pair
(\mathpzc W_{N},\mathscr{U}_{N})
is tight with respect to the topology of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).
Furthermore any joint limit point
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{U})
is uniquely characterized as follows:

(1)

If we define
\xi_{1}:=(2\sigma^{2})^{-1/2}\partial_{s}\mathcal{W}\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2}(\Psi_{T})
then
\xi_{1}
is a standard space-time white noise.

(2)

\mathcal{U}
is the solution to the Ito SPDE given by

\partial_{t}\mathcal{U}=\partial_{x}^{2}\mathcal{U}+\sqrt{8\sigma^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{U}\xi_{1}+\sqrt{\frac{32\sigma^{4}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}}\cdot\mathcal{U}\xi_{2},

where
\xi_{2}
is a standard space-time white noise independent of
\xi_{1}
.

Note that
\mathpzc W_{N}
is simply a time-integrated and linearly interpolated version of the rescaled noise field
\Xi_{N}
from (
1.5
). The above theorem readily implies Theorem
1.4
.

Proof.

We are going to consider the martingales
\mathpzc W_{N}(t,\phi)
as defined in (
6.21
), and we are going to study the cross-variations with the martingales
M_{N}(t,\phi)
from (
4.9
), both indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

First, let us note that if
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
then the process
\mathpzc W_{N}(t,\phi)
indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
has independent increments, where each increment has mean zero and has variance

4\sigma^{2}N^{-3/2}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{r-x-rN^{-1/4}\equiv 0\pmod{2}\}}.

From here (using BDG) the tightness of
\mathpzc W_{N}
in the space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
will follow easily from Lemma

6.17
(a)
. Since martingality is preserved by limit points as long as uniform integrability holds, one may verify that for any limit point
\mathcal{W}
, the processes
\mathcal{W}_{t}(\phi)
and
\mathcal{W}_{t}(\phi)^{2}-2\sigma^{2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}t
will be martingales in the canonical filtration of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
, for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. By Levy’s criterion,
\mathcal{W}
must therefore be distributed as
\sigma\sqrt{2}
times a standard cylindrical Wiener process over
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, that is, a mean-zero Gaussian process such that
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{W}_{t}(\phi)\mathcal{W}_{s}(\psi)]=2\sigma^{2}(s\wedge t)(\phi,\psi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Next, with the martingales
M_{N}(\phi)
from (
4.9
), we note that the processes

M_{N}(t,\phi)\mathpzc W_{N}(t,\phi)-\langle M_{N}(\phi),\mathpzc W_{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}

are martingales indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where the predictable quadratic variation is defined by

\displaystyle\langle M_{N}(\phi),\mathpzc W_{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r=1}^{Nt}\mathbb{E}\big[(M_{N}(r,\phi)-M_{N}(r-1,\phi))(\mathpzc W_{N}(r,\phi)-\mathpzc W_{N}(r-1,\phi))|\mathcal{F}_{r-1}]

(6.22)

\displaystyle=4\sigma^{2}N^{-3/4}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}-rN^{-1/4}}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)\cdot\big(\nabla_{N}\phi\big)(N^{-1/2}x)\cdot Z_{N}^{\omega}(r,x).

We used (
4.10
) and the fact that the
\eta_{N}({r,x})
are iid in the last equality.

Let us consider a joint limit point
(\mathcal{W},\mathscr{M},\mathcal{U})
of the triple
(\mathpzc W_{N},\widehat{M}_{N},\mathscr{U}_{N})
in the function space
C\big([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})^{3}\big)
, where we recall that
\widehat{M}_{N}
is the interpolated version of
M_{N}
from Definition
6.4
, and tightness of
\widehat{M}_{N},\mathscr{U}_{N}
in this topology was proved in Proposition
6.19
.

Since martingality is preserved by limit points as long as one has uniform integrability, we see that
\mathscr{M}_{t}(\phi)
and
\mathcal{W}_{t}(\phi)
are martingales in the canonical filtration of the space
C\big([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})^{3}\big)
. Recall from Section
4
that
\big(\nabla_{N}\phi\big)(N^{-1/2}x)=N^{-1/4}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)+O(N^{-1/2})
. Thus by (
6.22
) we see in the limit that

(6.23)

\mathscr{M}_{t}(\phi)\mathcal{W}_{t}(\phi)-4\sigma^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}_{s}(\phi^{2})ds

is also a martingale with respect to the canonical filtration. Indeed the difference between the sum in (
6.22
) and integral in (
6.23
) is easily controlled, since we proved tightness of
\mathscr{U}_{N}
in a topology given by the norm of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).

On the other hand, we know by Theorem
1.1
that
\mathcal{U}
solves (
1.4
) for some driving noise
\xi
which can be deterministically recovered from
\mathcal{U}
by the following argument. By (
6.15
) we have that

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}_{t}(\phi)-\mathscr{M}_{s}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)-\mathcal{U}_{s}(x))\phi(x)dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{U}_{u}(x)\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)dxdu

(6.24)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}}\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{U}_{u}(x)\phi(x)\xi(du,dx),

where the latter is an Ito-Walsh stochastic integral against the noise (which a priori may be defined on an enriched probability space), and the last equality is a standard result for the stochastic heat equation (
1.4
), see
[
Wal86
]
. Now we may define

\mathcal{B}_{t}(\phi):=\sqrt{\frac{1-4\sigma^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}_{u}(x)^{-1}\phi(x)\mathscr{M}(du,dx)

where the latter should be understood as an Ito-Walsh stochastic integral against the orthomartingale
\mathscr{M}
, as defined in
[
Wal86
]
. The above stochastic integral is well-defined by strict positivity of
\mathcal{U}

[
Mue91
]
. Then from Levy’s criterion, it is clear that
\mathcal{B}_{t}
is a standard cylindrical Wiener process (hence can be realized in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
, such that one has
\partial_{s}\mathcal{B}=\xi
. Thus
\xi
has been deterministically recovered from
\mathcal{U}
.

Define
\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{t}:=(\sigma\sqrt{2})^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{t}
, which is also a standard cylindrical Wiener process. Combining (
6.24
) with the fact that (
6.23
) is a martingale, one sees that

\mathcal{B}_{t}(\phi)\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{t}(\phi)-(1-4\sigma^{2})^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}t

is a martingale for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).
By Levy’s criterion, this is enough to imply that
(\mathcal{B}_{t},\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a
jointly
Gaussian process, such that both coordinates are standard cylindrical Wiener processes, and furthermore
\mathbb{E}[\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{t}(\phi)\mathcal{B}_{s}(\phi)]=(s\wedge t)(1-4\sigma^{2})^{1/2}\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.
By elementary algebraic manipulations of Gaussian variables, we may therefore write
\mathcal{B}=(1-4\sigma^{2})^{1/2}\overline{\mathcal{W}}+2\sigma\mathcal{X}
where
\mathcal{X}=(\mathcal{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a standard cylindrical Wiener process independent of
\overline{\mathcal{W}}
. Now the theorem is proved, setting
\xi_{1}=\partial_{s}\overline{\mathcal{W}}
and
\xi_{2}=\partial_{s}\mathcal{X}.

∎

With the theorem proved, we now discuss some different interpretations of the above result, which will be more heuristic than rigorous.

The term
\sqrt{\tfrac{32\sigma^{4}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}}\cdot\xi_{2}
appearing in the preceding theorem can be viewed as the contribution which causes the chaos expansion method to fail in the introduction, as this term precisely contains the
L^{2}
-mass which escapes into the tails of the chaos expansion. Indeed one expects that this piece which escapes will decouple and become independent of the remaining noise in the limit, just as we see in Theorem
6.26
.

Rather than “creation of independent noise,” there is an alternative interpretation of the above theorem. In the proof we showed
\mathbb{E}[\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{t}(\phi)\mathcal{B}_{s}(\phi)]=(s\wedge t)(1-4\sigma^{2})^{1/2}(\phi,\psi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.
Thus, an equivalent way of formulating Theorem
6.26
is that
\xi_{1},\xi
are jointly Gaussian such that

\mathbb{E}\big[(\xi_{1},\varphi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\xi,\varphi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\big]=(1-4\sigma^{2})^{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},

where
\xi
is the driving noise of
\mathcal{U}
as in (
1.4
). The above equality strongly suggests that the rescaled density field
\mathscr{U}_{N}
from (
1.3
) has a tendency to concentrate on certain favored sites, such that macroscopically these favored sites have Lebesgue density
1-4\sigma^{2}
on average. In the limit, the noise
\xi
is generated by only the weight variables
\omega_{t,x}
from these favored sites, in other words, the memory of only a proportion
1-4\sigma^{2}
of the weights is remembered in the limit. The concentration of the field
\mathscr{U}_{N}
inside these favored sites is the reason why we fail to see convergence in a space of continuous functions.

Thus we see that heuristically, the three pathological phenomena that we have observed in this paper all seem to be equivalent: the failure of the naive chaos expansion, the failure of Theorem
1.1
to be strengthened to a topology of continuous functions, and the creation of independent noise in the limiting SPDE. It may be interesting to seek a more precise statement establishing a rigorous correspondence between these phenomena.

7.
Results for the quenched tail field

Definition 7.1
.

Fix a realization of the environment variables
\omega
, as in Theorem
1.1
. For
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
we define the quenched tail field by

F_{N}(t,x):=N^{1/4}C_{N,t,x}\mathsf{P}^{\omega}\big(R(Nt)\geq N^{3/4}t+N^{1/2}x\big).

The main result of this section will be that the family
\{\log F_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
of space-time processes converges to the KPZ equation, in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions of pointwise values
(t,x)
, thus confirming the physics prediction of
[
BLD20
, Section 4]
.

Lemma 7.2
.

For sufficiently large
N
and for all
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)]\leq 4(\pi t)^{-1/2}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}_{RW^{(1)}_{\nu}}
denote the law of a simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
. One takes the annealed expectation over the quenched expectation in the above definition of
F_{N}
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)]

\displaystyle=N^{1/4}C_{N,t,x}\mathbf{P}(N^{-1/2}(S(Nt)-N^{3/4}t)\geq x)

\displaystyle=N^{1/4}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}[e^{-N^{-1/4}(S(Nt)-N^{3/4}t-N^{1/2}x)}\mathbf{1}_{\{N^{-1/2}(S(Nt)-N^{3/4}t)\geq x\}}].

where
\widehat{\mathbf{P}}
is a change of measure induced by the exponential martingale
C_{N,t,S(Nt)-N^{3/4}t},
which changes the annealed law of the increments of
S_{i}
from the usual symmetric law
\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{1}+\delta_{-1})
to the new law given by
\frac{1}{2\cosh(N^{-1/4})}(e^{N^{-1/4}}\delta_{1}+e^{-N^{-1/4}}\delta_{-1})
. Denote the new expectation as
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}.
To prove the inequality, we let
\widehat{p}_{N}(r,y)=\widehat{\mathbf{P}}(S(r)-N^{-1/4}r=y)
. Note that under the tilted measure
\widehat{\mathbf{P}}
, the increments of the random walk have mean
N^{-1/4}+O(N^{-3/4}).
Then the local central limit theorem implies that for sufficiently large
N
,

\sup_{y\in\mathbb{Z}-N^{-1/4}r}\widehat{p}_{N}(r,y)\leq 2(\pi r)^{-1/2}.

Consequently by setting
r=Nt
we see that

\displaystyle N^{1/4}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}[e^{-N^{-1/4}(S(Nt)-N^{3/4}t-N^{1/2}x)}\mathbf{1}_{\{N^{-1/2}(S(Nt)-N^{3/4}t)\geq x\}}]

\displaystyle=N^{1/4}\sum_{a\geq N^{1/2}x}\widehat{p}_{N}(Nt,a)e^{-N^{-1/4}(a-N^{1/2}x)}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\pi Nt)^{-1/2}N^{1/4}\sum_{a\geq N^{1/2}x}e^{-N^{-1/4}(a-N^{1/2}x)}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\pi t)^{-1/2}N^{-1/4}\sum_{b\geq 0}e^{-N^{-1/4}b}=2(\pi t)^{-1/2}\frac{N^{-1/4}}{1-e^{-N^{-1/4}}},

where the sums over
a
should be understood as being over
a\in\mathbb{Z}-N^{-1/4}Nt
with
a\geq N^{1/2}x
. The last quotient is always bounded above by 2, thus proving the claim.
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{m}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Consider sequences
t_{N,1},\ldots,t_{N,m}\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
t_{N,i}\to t_{i}>0
for all
1\leq i\leq m
as
N\to\infty.
Then

(7.1)

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)F_{N}(t_{N,i},x)dx\bigg)_{i=1}^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)\mathcal{U}_{t_{i}}(x)dx\bigg)_{i=1}^{m}.

Here
(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)
is the solution of (
1.4
).

Proof.

For
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
let us define a family of measures on
\mathbb{R}
given by

\mu_{N,t}:=e^{(N^{1/2}-N\log\cosh(N^{-1/4}))t}\sum_{x\in N^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}-N^{1/4}t}\mathsf{P}^{\omega}(Nt,N^{3/4}t+N^{1/2}x)\cdot\delta_{x},

Then we notice that Definition
7.1
is equivalent to

F_{N}(t,x):=N^{1/4}e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t}[x,\infty).

An application of Fubini’s theorem gives

\int_{-\infty}^{x}N^{1/4}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{N,t}[u,\infty)du=\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{N,t}(du)+e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t}[x,\infty).

In the sense of distributions, it is clear that

\displaystyle F_{N}(t,x)=\partial_{x}\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{x}N^{1/4}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{N,t}[u,\infty)du\bigg],\quad e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t}(dx)=\partial_{x}\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{N,t}(du)\bigg].

Consequently, for all
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we can repeatedly integrate by parts to obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(x)F_{N}(t,x)dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi^{\prime}(x)\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{x}N^{1/4}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{N,t}[u,\infty)du\bigg]dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi^{\prime}(x)\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{N,t}(du)+e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t}[x,\infty)\bigg]dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t}(dx)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi^{\prime}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t}[x,\infty)dx.

Now let us prove the proposition. In the setting of the proposition statement, by Theorem
1.1
we know that as
N\to\infty

\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t_{N,i}}(dx)\bigg)_{i=1}^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)\mathcal{U}_{t_{i}}(x)dx\bigg)_{i=1}^{m}.

Thus, to show (
7.1
) it suffices to show that as
N\to\infty
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t_{N,i}}[x,\infty)dx
goes to zero in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P})
for each
1\leq i\leq m
. Indeed, observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{N,t_{N,i}}[x,\infty)dx\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)|e^{N^{1/4}x}\mathbb{E}[\mu_{N,t_{N,i}}[x,\infty)]dx.

By Lemma
7.2
we get that

e^{N^{1/4}x}\mathbb{E}[\mu_{N,t_{N,i}}[x,\infty)]\leq 4N^{-1/4}(\pi t_{N,i})^{-1/2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Since the
t_{N,i}
s remain bounded away from
0
as
N\to\infty
, this implies the desired
L^{1}
convergence and completes the proof.
∎

Our next lemma shows uniform convergence of the two-point correlation function of the quenched tail field. This is the crucial result that allows us to improve Proposition
7.3
to obtain multipoint convergence in law of
F_{N}(t,x)
to
\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)
for
individual
values of
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose that
t_{N}\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
x_{N},y_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume that as
N\to\infty
we have
t_{N}\to t>0
and
(x_{N},y_{N})\to(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t_{N},x_{N})F_{N}(t_{N},y_{N})]=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)\mathcal{U}_{t}(y)]

where
\mathcal{U}
is as in (
1.4
).

Proof.

By taking an annealed expectation over the quenched one, notice that the two-point correlation function given by
(t,x,y)\mapsto\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)F_{N}(t,y)]
can be written as an expression involving only the 2-point motion
(R^{1},R^{2})
. More specifically, in the notation of Section
2.1
, it equals

Ne^{2(N^{1/2}-N\log\cosh(N^{-1/4}))t}\mathbf{P}_{RW^{(2)}_{\nu}}(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}({Nt})-N^{3/4}t)\geq x,N^{-1/2}(R^{2}(Nt)-N^{3/4}t)\geq y).

Consequently the two-point correlation function only depends on the mean and variance of the weight measure
\nu
. Without any loss of generality we shall henceforth assume
\nu
is the law of Beta
(\alpha,\alpha)
where
\alpha:=\frac{1-4\sigma^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}.

We shall now invoke results from
[
BC17
]
which are for the Beta measure. In
[
BC17
]
, the authors consider a slightly different field
\widehat{F}_{N}(t,x)
which is “dual” to ours in a certain sense. More specifically, they vary the starting point
x
and fix the tail probability as
[0,\infty)
, whereas we fix the starting point
0
and vary the tail probability as
[x,\infty)
. One may show that the distributions of both fields are the same as space-time processes, modulo a reflection of the weight measure. More precisely, we have
F_{N}^{\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\mu}
, where
\mu
is the pushforward of
\nu
by
x\mapsto 1-x
, and the superscript highlights the dependence of each field on the underlying weight measure.
1
1

1

In particular, our results imply the space-time multi-point convergence in law of their field
\widehat{F}_{N}
to (
1.4
) as well.
This is proved using an explicit coupling of the two fields via the “discrete Brownian web”. See
[
Yu16
, Equation (1.8)]
for a discussion of the coupling construction, which is in turn based on
[
SSS14
, Section 3]
.

Note that for the case of Beta
(\alpha,\alpha)
we have
\mu=\nu
. Proposition 3.4 in
[
BC17
]
provides exact moment formulas for the unscaled version of
F_{N}(t,x)
in this case. Taking the scalings into account (see e.g.
[
BLD20
, (34) and (40)-(43)]
) we then have for all
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in N^{-1/2}(\mathbb{Z}-N^{3/4}t)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x_{1})F_{N}(t,x_{2})]

\displaystyle=N^{1/2}\oint_{\gamma_{1}}\oint_{\gamma_{2}}\frac{z_{1}-z_{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}-1}\prod_{j=1}^{2}C_{N,t,x_{j}}\bigg(\frac{2\alpha+z_{j}}{z_{j}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}(Nt-N^{\frac{3}{4}}t-x_{j}N^{\frac{1}{2}})+1}\bigg(\frac{\alpha+z_{j}}{2\alpha+z_{j}}\bigg)^{Nt}\frac{1}{2\alpha+z_{j}}\frac{dz_{1}}{2\pi i}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i},

where
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
are positively oriented closed curves around 0 such that
\gamma_{1}
contains
\gamma_{2}+1

and both contours exclude
-2\alpha
.

Now assume that we have a sequence
(t_{N},x_{N},y_{N})
converging to
(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We claim that the integral expression for
\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t_{N},x_{N})F_{N}(t_{N},y_{N})]
converges as
N\to\infty
to

\alpha^{-2}\oint_{r_{1}+i\mathbb{R}}\oint_{r_{2}+i\mathbb{R}}\frac{z_{2}-z_{1}}{z_{2}-z_{1}-1}e^{\frac{t}{2\alpha^{2}}(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2})+\frac{1}{\alpha}(xz_{1}+yz_{2})}\frac{dz_{1}}{2\pi i}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}.

Here
r_{1},r_{2}
are chosen such that
r_{2}>r_{1}+1.
This convergence can be justified by dominated convergence and Taylor expansions, see
[
BC14
, Proposition 5.4.2]
for a similar argument.
This is known to agree with
\mathbf{E}[\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)\mathcal{U}_{t}(y)]
, see
[
BC14
, Section 6.2]
, thus proving the lemma.
∎

With the above lemma in place, we prove our main convergence result for the quenched tail field: Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

We first prove the case when
x_{N,i}=x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. We give a proof for
m=1
to simplify the notation, but the generalization to larger
m
is straightforward. We will simply write
(t_{N,1},x_{1})
as
(t_{N},x)
, which will be fixed for the moment being. By Lemma
7.2
we get that
\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t_{N},x)]\leq 2(\pi t_{N})^{-1/2}
which is bounded independently of
N
, thus it follows that the
\{F_{N}(t_{N},x)\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight. Consider any limit point
\mu_{0}
of the laws of
\{F_{N}(t_{N},x)\}_{N\geq 1}
. Fix a smooth compactly supported nonnegative function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
which integrates to
1
, and define
\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)=\lambda^{-1}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(y-x)).
By Proposition
7.3
we know that for each
\lambda,x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)F_{N}(t_{N},y)dy\big\}_{N\geq 1}
is a tight sequence. For
r\geq 1
, consider any measure
\mu_{r}
on
\mathbb{R}^{r+1}
which is a joint limit point as
N\to\infty
of

\bigg(F_{N}(t_{N},x)\ ,\ \big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi^{2^{-k}}_{x}(y)F_{N}(t_{N},y)dy\big)_{k=1}^{r}\bigg),

such that the first marginal of
\mu_{r}
is
\mu_{0}
. Using a diagonalization argument, these measures
\mu_{r}
may be chosen so that they form a projective family, and therefore by the Kolmogorov extension theorem, we may consider any projective limit
\mu
as
r\to\infty
, which will be a measure on the space of sequences
(a_{k})_{k\geq 0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
, equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the projection maps. By Proposition
7.3
, we find that for such a measure
\mu
the marginal distribution of
(a_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is simply equal to the law of
\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{U}_{t}(y)\phi^{2^{-k}}_{x}(y)dy\big)_{k\geq 1}
. We now claim that

(7.2)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\lambda\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg|F_{N}(t_{N},x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{N}(t_{N},y)\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy\bigg|=0.

Assuming this fact, one finds that such a measure
\mu
is necessarily supported on those sequences
(a_{k})_{k\geq 0}
which satisfy
a_{0}=\lim_{k\to\infty}a_{k}
in
L^{1}(\mu)
, which means that
a_{0}
must indeed have the law of
\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)
under
\mu
, proving the lemma. We are thus left to check (
7.2
).
By Lemma
7.4
, we have that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[(F_{N}(t,x)-F_{N}(t,y))^{2}]=\mathbf{E}[(\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)-\mathcal{U}_{t}(y))^{2}]\leq\mathrm{C}|x-y|

uniformly over compacts sets of
(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
where the above inequality is a known estimate for (
1.4
) (see
[
Das22
, Proposition 2.4-nw]
for example). Thus, by Fatou’s lemma, we note that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg|F_{N}(t_{N},x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{N}(t_{N},y)\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}|F_{N}(t_{N},x)-F_{N}(t_{N},y)|\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}|F_{N}(t_{N},x)-F_{N}(t_{N},y)|\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[(F_{N}(t_{N},x)-F_{N}(t_{N},y))^{2}]^{1/2}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x-y|^{1/2}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy=\mathrm{C}\lambda^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u|^{1/2}\phi(u)du.

Taking
\lambda\to 0
leads to (
7.2
). To justify the above application of Fatou’s lemma with
\limsup
, one needs a bound on the
y
-integrand which is independent of
N
and is in
L^{1}(\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy)
. For this we may use Lemma
7.2
to get

\mathbb{E}|F_{N}(t_{N},x)-F_{N}(t_{N},y)|\leq\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t_{N},x)+F_{N}(t_{N},y)]\leq 2(2\pi t_{N})^{-1/2}.

The right side may be bounded by a constant independent of
N
since
t_{N}\to t>0
. Finally, the case when
x_{N,i}
varies with
N
follows from the fact that as
N\to\infty
we have

\mathbb{E}\big[\big(F_{N}(t_{N,i},x_{N,i})-F_{N}(t_{N,i},x_{i})\big)^{2}\big]\to 0

due to the uniform convergence in Lemma
7.4
. This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 7.5

(Extremal particle limit theorem)
.

Fix
c,t>0
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
(R^{1}(r),\ldots,R^{k}(r))_{r\geq 0}
denote the canonical process sampled from the measure
\mathbf{P}_{RW_{\nu}^{(k)}}.
Set the number of particles
k=k(N):=\lfloor\exp(\frac{1}{2}cN^{1/2}+dN^{1/4}+r_{N})\rfloor
where
r_{N}
can be any sequence satisfying
r_{N}=o(N^{1/4})
. Consider any sequence
t_{N}\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
t_{N}\to t>0
as
N\to\infty
. Then

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq k(N)}\big\{N^{-\frac{1}{4}}R^{i}(Nt_{N})\big\}-a_{N}(c,d,t_{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sqrt{\tfrac{t}{c}}\big(G+\log\mathcal{U}_{c}(d)\big),

where

\displaystyle a_{N}(c,d,s):=\sqrt{csN}-dN^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{\tfrac{s}{c}}-\sqrt{\tfrac{c}{s}}\big(r_{N}-\tfrac{1}{4}\log N\big)-\frac{c^{3/2}}{6\sqrt{s}}.

Here
G
is a standard Gumbel random variable which is independent of
\mathcal{U}
, and
(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)
solves (
1.4
) but with noise coefficient replaced by
\sqrt{\frac{8\sigma^{2}c}{(1-4\sigma^{2})t}}
.

Proof.

Fix any
a\in\mathbb{R}
.
Set
v_{N}:=\sqrt{ct_{N}}
and
x_{N}:=d\sqrt{\frac{t_{N}}{c}}
so that
c=\frac{v_{N}^{2}}{t_{N}}
and
d=v_{N}x_{N}/t_{N}
. Also define
v=\sqrt{ct}
and
x=d\sqrt{\frac{t}{c}}
. It suffices to show

(7.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{P}_{RW^{(k(N))}_{\nu}}\bigg(\max_{1\leq i\leq k(N)}\big\{R^{i}(Nt_{N})\big\}-v_{N}N^{3/4}-x_{N}\sqrt{N}-\tfrac{tN^{1/4}}{v_{N}}\big(r_{N}-\tfrac{1}{4}\log N\big)\leq aN^{1/4}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(t/v[G+\log\mathcal{U}_{v^{2}/t}(vx/t)]\leq a+\frac{v^{3}}{6t^{2}}\bigg),

where
\mathcal{U}
solves (
1.4
) with noise coefficient replaced by
\frac{v}{t}\sqrt{\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}}
, and
G
is an independent Gumbel random variable.

For now, fix any
k\in\mathbb{N}
which may be arbitrary. Fix a realization of the environment
\omega=\{\omega_{i,j}:(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}.
As before, we let
\mathsf{P}_{(k)}^{\omega}
be the quenched probability of
k
random motions
(R^{i}(r))_{i=1}^{k}
sampled given the environment. Let us consider the random variable

(7.4)

\mathsf{P}_{(k)}^{\omega}\bigg(\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\big\{R^{i}(Nt_{N})\big\}\leq v_{N}N^{3/4}+x_{N}\sqrt{N}+\frac{t_{N}}{v_{N}}N^{1/4}(r_{N}-\frac{1}{4}\log N)+aN^{1/4}\bigg).

Notice that until this point, we have assumed in Theorem
1.1
that the parameter
N
is a
discrete
parameter taking values in
\mathbb{N}
. However nothing in our proof actually used this discreteness, and in fact, there is no loss of generality in assuming it is actually a
continuous
parameter indexed by
[1,\infty)
for instance. In this context, the results of Theorem
1.1
and therefore Theorem
1.2
still hold as
N\to\infty
.

Now we define
\mathfrak{N}=\mathfrak{N}(N):=N\big(\frac{t_{N}}{v_{N}}\big)^{4}
, so that
\mathfrak{N}\to\infty
is equivalent to
N\to\infty
. Notice by Definition
7.1
that (
7.4
) is pathwise equal to the quantity

(7.5)

\displaystyle\bigg(1-\mathfrak{N}^{-1/4}C_{\mathfrak{N},\tfrac{v_{N}^{4}}{t_{N}^{3}},\big(\tfrac{v_{N}}{t_{N}}\big)^{2}x_{N}+\mathfrak{N}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(\tfrac{av_{N}}{t_{N}}+r_{N}-\tfrac{1}{4}\log N)}^{-1}F_{\mathfrak{N}}\big(\tfrac{v_{N}^{4}}{t_{N}^{3}},\;\big(\tfrac{v_{N}}{t_{N}}\big)^{2}x_{N}+\mathfrak{N}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(\tfrac{av_{N}}{t_{N}}+r_{N}-\tfrac{1}{4}\log N)\big)\bigg)^{k}.

Then we may choose

\displaystyle k=k(N)

\displaystyle=\bigg\lfloor\mathfrak{N}^{1/4}C_{\mathfrak{N},\tfrac{v_{N}^{4}}{t_{N}^{3}},\big(\tfrac{v_{N}}{t_{N}}\big)^{2}x_{N}+\mathfrak{N}^{-\frac{1}{4}}(\tfrac{av_{N}}{t_{N}}+r_{N}-\tfrac{1}{4}\log N))}\exp\bigg[-\frac{v_{N}^{4}}{6t_{N}^{3}}-a\frac{v_{N}}{t_{N}}-\log(t_{N}/v_{N})\bigg]\bigg\rfloor

\displaystyle=\bigg\lfloor\exp\bigg[\frac{v_{N}^{2}}{2t_{N}}\sqrt{N}+\frac{v_{N}x_{N}}{t_{N}}N^{1/4}+r_{N}+O(N^{-1/2})\bigg]\bigg\rfloor

where we applied a fourth order Taylor expansion of
\log\cosh
in order to deduce the
O(N^{-1/2})
term. Note that the latter expression agrees with the choice of
k(N)
in the theorem statement. Then (
7.5
) is a quantity of the form
(1-\frac{u(N)}{k(N)+O_{N}(1)})^{k(N)}
, where the
O_{N}(1)
term is deterministic and bounded between
0
and
1
, and
u(N)
is a sequence of random variables which by Proposition
1.2
converges in law to the strictly positive random variable
\tfrac{v}{t}e^{-\frac{av}{t}-\frac{v^{4}}{6t^{3}}}\mathcal{U}_{v^{4}/t^{3}}(v^{2}x/t^{2})
with
\mathcal{U}
solving (
1.4
). The latter has the same law as
e^{-\frac{av}{t}-\frac{v^{4}}{6t^{3}}}\mathcal{U}_{v^{2}/t}(vx/t)
where now the noise coefficient in (
1.4
) has been replaced by
\frac{v}{t}\sqrt{\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}}
. Since the functions
u\mapsto(1-\frac{u}{k(N)+O_{N}(1)})^{k(N)}
converge uniformly to
u\mapsto e^{-u}
on compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
, we can conclude that the expression in (
7.5
) therefore converges in law to the random variable

\exp\big[-e^{-\frac{av}{t}-\frac{v^{4}}{6t^{3}}}\mathcal{U}_{v^{2}/t}(vx/t)\big],

where
\mathcal{U}
solves (
1.4
) with noise coefficient replaced by
\frac{v}{t}\sqrt{\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}}
. Now this convergence in law also implies convergence of the associated annealed expectations thanks to the boundedness of all quantities involved, which implies that the limit of the left side of (
7.3
) equals

\mathbb{E}\bigg[-e^{-\frac{av}{t}-\frac{v^{4}}{6t^{3}}}\mathcal{U}_{v^{2}/t}(vx/t)\bigg],

which agrees with the right-hand side of (
7.3
) by the explicit form of the Gumbel distribution.
∎

Note that in the above theorem, a natural choice is
t_{N}:=N^{-1}\lfloor Nt\rfloor
where
t>0
is fixed. However, we also have the liberty to set
t_{N}:=N^{-1}\lfloor Nt+N^{\alpha}\rfloor,
where
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and this would have a nontrivial effect in the recentering coefficients
a_{N}(c,d,t_{N})
if
\alpha\in[1/2,1)
.

Probability measures and filtrations

\nu

Probability measure on
[0,1]
representing law of each

Variance of
\nu

Sec.
2.1

\mathbf{P}_{RW_{\nu}^{(k)}}

Probability measure on the path space
(\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}},
used to denote the annealed law of
k
independent walks sampled from the environment
\omega
whose weights are distributed as
\nu
, or more precisely
\mathbf{P}_{RW_{\nu}^{(k)}}=\mathbb{P}\mathsf{P}^{\omega}_{(k)}

Def.
2.2

\mathsf{P}^{\omega}_{(k)}

Probability measure on the path space
(\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
, used to denote the quenched law of
k
independent walks sampled from the fixed realization of environment
\omega=\{\omega_{t,x}\}_{t,x}

\mathbb{P}

Probability measure on the environment space
[0,1]^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}}
, used to denote the annealed probability associated to any observable on the probability space of the environment
\{\omega_{t,x}\}_{t,x}

\mathbf{P}_{\text{lim}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\infty}

Notation used for limit points of objects on the canonical space of continuous paths (or tuples of continuous paths)

(\mathcal{F}_{r})_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

(\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
